Well-posedness for the periodic Intermediate nonlinear Schrödinger equation
Andreia Chapouto, Justin Forlano, Thierry Laurens

Abstract
. We study the well-posedness for the intermediate nonlinear Schrödinger equation (INLS) with periodic boundary conditions. Using a gauge transform, we obtain large data local well-posedness in H s ​ ( 𝕋 ) H^{s}(\mathbb{T}) for any s ≥ 1 2 s\geq\frac{1}{2} . We extend this result to global well-posedness under a small L 2 L^{2} -norm constraint by exploiting the complete integrability of the continuum Calogero-Moser equation (CCM). We also establish additional results such as the unconditional well-posedness in the energy space and the convergence of solutions to INLS to those of CCM in the infinite-depth limit.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
35Q53, 35A02, 76B55

1.
Introduction

1.1.
The intermediate nonlinear Schrödinger equation

We consider the Cauchy problem for the periodic intermediate nonlinear Schrödinger equation (INLS):

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}u+i\partial_{x}^{2}u=\beta u(1+i\mathcal{T}_{h})\partial_{x}(|u|^{2})+i\gamma|u|^{2}u,\\
&u|_{t=0}=u_{0},\end{aligned}\right.

(1.1)

where
u:\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{C}
,
\mathbb{T}=\mathbb{R}/(2\pi\mathbb{Z})
,
0<h<\infty
, and
\gamma,\beta\in\mathbb{R}
.
Here, the operator
\mathcal{T}_{h}
is a Fourier multiplier operator with symbol

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{T}_{h}f}(\xi)=-i\coth(h\xi)\widehat{f}(\xi),\quad\xi\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\},

and we define
\widehat{\mathcal{T}_{h}f}(0)=0
.
1
1

1

We use the notation
\widehat{f}
for the Fourier transform of
f
; see (
2.1
).

The sign of
\beta
in (
1.1
) determines the defocusing (
\beta>0
) or focusing (
\beta<0
) variety of this equation.

The equation (
1.1
) was proposed in
[
54
,
56
]
as a model for the evolution of quasi-harmonic internal waves in a two-fluid layer system, where the bottom fluid is of a finite depth
h>0
, and with higher order effects accounted for by
\gamma\neq 0
.
Additionally, (
1.1
) with
\gamma=0
and on
\mathbb{R}
has a rich structure: it is completely integrable
[
55
,
10
]
, possesses multi-soliton solutions
[
54
,
60
]
, and is closely related to two other equations of great interest, as we now discuss.

Motivated by the observation that
\mathcal{T}_{h}
formally converges to the Hilbert transform
\mathcal{H}
as
h\to\infty
, i.e., that
-i\coth(h\xi)\to-i\textup{sgn}(\xi)
as
h\to\infty
for fixed
\xi\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}
,
we rewrite (
1.1
) as

\displaystyle\partial_{t}u+i\partial_{x}^{2}u=\beta u(1+i\mathcal{H})\partial_{x}(|u|^{2})-i\beta u\mathcal{G}_{h}(|u|^{2})+i\gamma|u|^{2}u,

(1.2)

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{h}:=(\mathcal{H}-\mathcal{T}_{h})\partial_{x}.

(1.3)

Now, (
1.2
) admits further rewriting, for which we need to define some frequency projectors. Given a set
A\subseteq\mathbb{Z}
, we write
\mathbf{1}_{A}
for the characteristic function of the set
A
. We define
\mathbf{P}_{\pm}
as the Fourier multiplier with symbol
\mathbf{1}_{\{\pm\xi>0\}}
, so that on
\mathbb{T}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}_{+}+\mathbf{P}_{0}+\mathbf{P}_{-}=\text{Id},

where
\mathbf{P}_{0}
is the projection onto the zeroth Fourier mode:

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}_{0}f=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{T}}f(x)dx.

We define
\mathbf{P}_{\neq 0}:=\text{Id}-\mathbf{P}_{0}
.
With these notations, the Hilbert transform is given by
\mathcal{H}=-i\mathbf{P}_{+}+i\mathbf{P}_{-}
and thus
(1+i\mathcal{H})=2\mathbf{P}_{+}+\mathbf{P}_{0}
.
Thus, (
1.4
) becomes:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}u+i\partial_{x}^{2}u=2\beta u\mathbf{P}_{+}\partial_{x}(|u|^{2})-i\beta u\mathcal{G}_{h}(|u|^{2})+i\gamma|u|^{2}u,

(1.4)

since
\mathbf{P}_{0}\partial_{x}=0
on
\mathbb{T}
.
Now, formally taking the
infinite depth limit

h\to\infty
of (
1.4
), we find

\displaystyle\partial_{t}u+i\partial_{x}^{2}u=2\beta u\mathbf{P}_{+}\partial_{x}(|u|^{2})+i\gamma|u|^{2}u.

(1.5)

It is then natural to define
\mathcal{G}_{\infty}:=0
so that we may speak of (
1.4
) for all
0<h\leq\infty
.
In the special case
\gamma=0
, we obtain the continuum Calogero-Moser equation (CCM)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}u+i\partial_{x}^{2}u=2\beta u\mathbf{P}_{+}\partial_{x}(|u|^{2}),

(1.6)

which is also known as the Calogero-Sutherland derivative nonlinear Schrödinger equation.
On
\mathbb{R}
, the (focusing) CCM arises as a suitable thermodynamic limit of the
N
-body Calogero-Sutherland-Moser system
[
1
]
.

From the well-posedness viewpoint, scaling considerations identify
L^{2}
as a critical space for (
1.6
). This suggests that, within the scope of
L^{2}
-based Sobolev space
H^{s}
, it is necessary to have
s\geq 0
in order for (
1.6
) to be well-posed, and we expect the same to hold true for (
1.4
).
Some evidence for this is that the solution map for (
1.4
), if it exists, cannot be
C^{3}
at the origin in
H^{s}(\mathbb{R})
for any
s<0

[
53
]
.
The investigations of well-posedness of (
1.6
) have been accelerated by the observation that it preserves the Hardy space
L^{2}_{+}
, which is defined as

\displaystyle L^{2}_{+}

\displaystyle=\big\{f\in L^{2}:\ \supp\widehat{f}\subset[0,\infty)\big\}.

In particular, global well-posedness for large (resp. small) data in the defocusing (resp. focusing) setting has recently been established in the Hardy space
L^{2}_{+}
on
\mathbb{T}

[
3
]
and on
\mathbb{R}

[
37
]
. This was revisited in
[
38
]
on both geometries using the method of commuting flows; see
[
53
,
19
,
6
,
26
]
for prior works.
The results
[
3
,
37
]
both exploit the complete integrability of (
1.6
) through an explicit formula for solutions in the Hardy space, similar to that for the Benjamin-Ono equation (BO) discovered by Gérard
[
25
]
.
The explicit formulae have proven very useful beyond well-posedness, such as in the study of travelling waves
[
5
]
, zero dispersion limits
[
4
]
, scattering with non-vanishing background
[
13
]
, soliton resolution for BO
[
23
]
, numerical schemes
[
2
]
, and blow-up solutions
[
15
]
.
Unfortunately, it is not known if such an explicit formula holds for (
1.6
) outside of the Hardy space, and if at all for (
1.1
).
Thus, in order to study (
1.1
), we pursue an approach which does not rely on complete integrability.

For CCM outside of the Hardy space and
for (
1.1
), less is understood regarding the low-regularity well-posedness. On
\mathbb{R}
, using dispersive properties of the linear part of (
1.1
) and a gauge transform, de Moura-Pilod
[
19
]
proved local well-posedness for (
1.1
) in
H^{s}(\mathbb{R})
for
s>\frac{1}{2}
. See also
[
18
,
53
,
6
]
.
Very recently, in
[
10
]
,
we lowered this regularity condition to
s>\frac{1}{4}
.
In contrast, key techniques employed in the line setting do not extend to the periodic setting, as they are based on smoothing effects (e.g. local smoothing and bilinear Strichartz estimates), which are absent on
\mathbb{T}
.
One can still apply the standard energy method on
\mathbb{T}
, to cover
H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
for
s>\frac{3}{2}
; see for instance
[
19
, Section 5]
which can be adapted to the circle case. However, going beyond this result requires a different approach, which is the main goal of this work.

Before we move onto discussing our results, we want to point out that for the long-time behaviour of energy class solutions for CCM on
\mathbb{R}
, even outside of the Hardy space, there have been remarkable results regarding soliton resolution
[
39
]
, and blow-up solutions and classification of blow-up rates
[
31
,
40
,
34
,
35
]
. In the periodic setting, traveling waves have been studied in
[
5
,
16
,
45
,
46
,
47
]
.
Another approach to understanding the long-time qualitative behaviour in the periodic case would be to study (
1.1
) in statistical equilibrium; namely, to construct strong solutions on the support of the associated invariant Gibbs measure and apply the Poincaré recurrence theorem.
For (
1.1
), the samples of the Gibbs measure would be supported in
H^{s}(\mathbb{T})\setminus H^{\frac{1}{2}}(\mathbb{T})
for any
s<\frac{1}{2}
almost surely, which is just beyond the results we can prove here and we leave this interesting question to a future work.

1.2.
Main results

Our first main result is on the local well-posedness of (
1.1
) and its unconditional uniqueness.

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
s\geq\frac{1}{2}
,
0<h\leq\infty
, and
\beta,\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
. Then,
INLS
(
1.1
) is locally well-posed in
H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
. More precisely, for any
u_{0}\in H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
, there exist
T_{h}=T(\|u_{0}\|_{H^{s}},h)>0
and a unique solution

\displaystyle u\in Z^{s}_{T_{h}}:=C([-T_{h},T_{h}];H^{s}(\mathbb{T}))\cap L^{4}_{T_{h}}W^{s,4}_{x}\cap X^{s-1,1}_{T_{h}}

(1.7)

to (
1.1
) in
[-T_{h},T_{h}]
with the property that

\displaystyle\widetilde{v}(t,x):=\mathbf{P}_{\textup{+,hi}}[e^{i\beta F[u]}u](t,x-2\beta\mathbf{P}_{0}(|u_{0}|^{2})t)\in X^{s,\frac{1}{2}+}_{T_{h}}\quad\text{and}\quad w:=\mathbf{P}_{-,\textup{hi}}u\in X^{s,\frac{1}{2}+}_{T_{h}},

(1.8)

where
F
is the primitive of
\mathbf{P}_{\neq 0}(|u|^{2})
; see (
3.4
), and the spaces
X^{s,b}_{T}
are defined in Section
3.1
.
Here
T_{h}>0
can be chosen uniformly in
1\leq h\leq\infty
.
Furthermore, the solution map
u_{0}\mapsto u
is continuous from
H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
to
Z^{s}_{T_{h}}

and the solution
u
is unique in the class:

(i)

u\in L^{\infty}((0,T_{h});H^{\frac{1}{2}}(\mathbb{T}))\cap L^{4}((0,T_{h});W^{\frac{1}{2},4}_{x}(\mathbb{T}))
with
\widetilde{v},w\in X^{\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}+}_{T_{h}}
;

(ii)

u\in L^{\infty}((0,T_{h});H^{s}(\mathbb{T}))\cap L^{4}((0,T_{h});W^{s,4}_{x}(\mathbb{T}))
if
s>\frac{3}{5}
;

(iii)

u\in L^{\infty}((0,T_{h});H^{s}(\mathbb{T}))
if
s>\frac{17}{20}
.

Theorem
1.1
establishes the local well-posedness of (
1.4
) in
H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
for any
s\geq\frac{1}{2}
, which is more than a whole derivative of improvement compared to the previous best result of
s>\frac{3}{2}

[
19
]
.
In proving Theorem
1.1
, we employ Fourier analytic arguments using a frequency-localized gauge transform which weakens the problematic
\text{low}\times\text{high}\to\text{high}
interactions in the nonlinear term
u\mathbf{P}_{+}\partial_{x}(|u|^{2})
. The gauge transform is a modification to the periodic setting of the one used on the line
[
19
,
10
]
, which itself goes back to the gauge transform introduced by Tao
[
58
]
for BO.
The overall structure of the proof follows that of Molinet-Pilod
[
50
]
in their study of BO in
L^{2}
, involving a bootstrap argument and the Fourier restriction norm spaces of Bourgain
[
8
]
. These spaces are well-adapted to the periodic setting allowing us to gain additional smoothing through multilinear dispersion, which is the key for the main trilinear estimates in Proposition
4.1
.
However, we encounter some new fundamental difficulties, absent in both
[
50
]
and in the setting of the line
\mathbb{R}
from our previous work
[
10
]
. We postpone this discussion to Subsection
1.3
.

We point out that Theorem
1.1
(iii) states that uniqueness holds
unconditionally
in
L^{\infty}_{T_{h}}H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
for any
s>\frac{17}{20}
; namely,
without intersecting with any auxiliary function space.
The concept of unconditional uniqueness goes back to Kato
[
36
]
and it allows to compare
L^{\infty}_{T}H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
-solutions irrespective of how they were constructed. In comparison, the uniqueness statements in Theorem
1.1
(i)-(ii), when
\frac{1}{2}\leq s\leq\frac{17}{20}
, involve more restrictive classes. The unconditional uniqueness issue has been studied by many authors in the past decades with various methods developed. See for instance
[
62
,
28
,
14
,
41
]
for a sampling of results.

We do not expect the range
s>\frac{17}{20}
to be sharp for unconditional uniqueness. Indeed, the ideal range occurs whenever
u\in L^{\infty}_{T}H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
is a distributional solution to (
1.1
), then the nonlinearity is well-defined as a distribution in space-time. For (
1.1
), or more specifically, the rewritten version (
1.4
), we need to make sense of
u\mathbf{P}_{+}\partial_{x}(|u|^{2})
and
|u|^{2}u
. The former is clearly more restrictive and requires
s>\frac{1}{2}
, which is a consequence of the Moser estimate:

\displaystyle\|fg\|_{H^{s}}\leq\|f\|_{L^{\infty}}\|g\|_{H^{s}}+\|f\|_{H^{s}}\|g\|_{L^{\infty}}\quad\text{for}\quad s\geq 0.

(1.9)

See Remark
1.7
below on the endpoint
s=\frac{1}{2}
.
Remarkably, the situation is vastly improved for the CCM equation (
1.6
) in the Hardy space. Indeed, we have the estimate

\displaystyle\|(\mathbf{P}_{+}f_{1})\mathbf{P}_{+}\partial_{x}(\overline{f_{2}}f_{3})\|_{H^{-3}}\lesssim\|f_{1}\|_{L^{2}}\|f_{2}\|_{L^{2}}\|f_{3}\|_{L^{2}},

(1.10)

which suggests that unconditional uniqueness of solutions to (
1.6
) in
L^{\infty}_{T}L^{2}_{+}(\mathbb{T})
may hold. This would be especially interesting as it corresponds to the scaling critical regularity.

Conjecture 1.2
.

The defocusing CCM equation (
1.6
) is unconditionally globally well-posed in
L^{2}_{+}(\mathbb{T})
and
L^{2}_{+}(\mathbb{R})
.

The unconditional uniqueness result in Theorem
1.1
also applies when restricted to CCM in the Hardy space and presents the first progress towards Conjecture
1.2
.

Our interest in our range for unconditional uniqueness in Theorem
1.1
is that it covers the energy space at
s=1
. Indeed, the equation (
1.1
) has the conservation law:

\displaystyle E(u)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{T}}\big\{|\partial_{x}u|^{2}-\beta|u|^{2}\text{Im}(u\partial_{x}\overline{u})+\tfrac{\beta}{2}|u|^{2}\mathcal{T}_{h}\partial_{x}(|u|^{2})+\tfrac{\gamma}{2}|u|^{4}+\tfrac{\beta^{2}}{3}|u|^{6}\Big\}dx.

(1.11)

Although the second term in (
1.11
) is not sign-definite, we can still use
E(u)
to obtain a priori bounds in
H^{1}(\mathbb{T})
for solutions to (
1.1
), which combined with Theorem
1.1
lead to the following result.

Corollary 1.3
.

Let
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
,
r>0
, and
B_{r}(0)=\{f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T})\,:\,\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\leq r\}
.
Then, the defocusing (
1.1
) is unconditionally globally well-posed in
H^{1}(\mathbb{T})
, and the focusing (
1.1
) is unconditionally globally well-posed in
H^{1}(\mathbb{T})\cap B_{r}(0)

for some
r>0
sufficiently small.

We point out the bounds we obtain to prove Corollary
1.3
do not depend upon the sign of
\gamma
(and only on the sign of
\beta
). This seems to be the first time that this has been observed in the literature
[
18
,
6
]
, where
\gamma\geq 0
was previously imposed.
The small
L^{2}
-restriction in the focusing case has very recently been shown to be necessary for the focusing CCM
[
15
]
, as otherwise, blowup can occur.

The next natural question we consider is the globalization of solutions
below
the energy space. It appears again that there is a dichotomy in the behaviour between even CCM (
1.6
) in and outside of the Hardy space. Let us consider the defocusing case
(\beta>0)
. Using the complete integrability of CCM in
L^{2}_{+}(\mathbb{T})
, Baddredine
[
3
]
obtained a priori bounds for solutions in
H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
for any
0\leq s\leq 1
. Outside of the Hardy space, however, it appears that the conservation laws lose their efficacy. For example, the momentum

\displaystyle P(u)=\int_{\mathbb{T}}\text{Im}(\overline{u}\partial_{x}{u})+\tfrac{\beta}{2}|u|^{4}dx

(1.12)

is conserved for (
1.6
) (and also for solutions to (
1.1
) with
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
) but alas the first term in (
1.12
) is not coercive. However, in the Hardy space, it can be written as

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}}\text{Im}(\overline{u}\partial_{x}{u})dx=\int_{\mathbb{T}}||\partial_{x}|^{\frac{1}{2}}u|^{2}dx

which is now coercive and, consequently,

P(u)
gives control over the
\dot{H}^{\frac{1}{2}}(\mathbb{T})
norm of
u
.
Despite the apparent lack of coercivity, we showed in
[
10
]
that INLS (
1.1
) on
\mathbb{R}
with
\gamma=0
has a Lax pair
outside
of the Hardy space which can be used to obtain a priori bounds in
H^{s}(\mathbb{R})
for any
s\geq\frac{1}{4}
, under a small mass constraint (even in the defocusing case).

Returning to our current setting on
\mathbb{T}
, the straightforward approach of mimicking the line case, namely, taking the same pair of operators
(\mathcal{L}_{u;h},\mathcal{P}_{u;h})
, modulo trivial redefinition of
\mathcal{T}_{h}
, and showing that they form a Lax pair on
\mathbb{T}

fails
.
In fact,
(\mathcal{L}_{u;h},\mathcal{P}_{u;h})
fail to be a Lax pair due to the presence of zeroth Fourier modes on
\mathbb{T}
; see Proposition
8.1
.
This failure stems from the Cotlar identity (
8.5
) for
\mathcal{T}_{h}
on
\mathbb{T}
being slightly different to that on
\mathbb{R}
, requiring mean-zero assumptions on the functions.
Note that the mean
\mathbf{P}_{0}u
is not preserved for solutions to (
1.1
), so we cannot restrict to mean zero functions to avoid this issue.

Nonetheless, we are able to obtain low-regularity a priori bounds for solutions to (
1.1
) with
\gamma=0
by using
only
the complete integrability of the CCM equation (
1.6
).
This allows us to extend Corollary
1.3
as follows.

Theorem 1.4
.

Let
\frac{1}{2}\leq s<1
,
0<h\leq\infty
,
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
, and
\gamma=0
. Then, there exists
r>0
such that INLS (
1.1
) is globally well-posed in
H^{s}(\mathbb{T})\cap B_{r}(0)
.

Similar to our previous work on
\mathbb{R}

[
10
]
, we need to impose a small
L^{2}
-norm assumption even in the defocusing case to run a bootstrap argument; see Theorem
8.4
. This seems to go back to the
L^{2}
-criticality of (
1.1
). We currently do not know if this can be removed in the defocusing case.
Additionally, we have to make the stronger regularity assumption
s\geq\frac{1}{2}
(compared to
s>\frac{1}{4}
on
\mathbb{R}
), which we now explain.
Our argument is inspired by the strategy for obtaining a priori bounds for perturbations of completely integrable equations in
[
11
]
, where the authors obtained a priori bounds for dispersive perturbations of the BO equation. See also
[
24
,
32
]
. The main idea there is to define some quantity which is conserved for the unperturbed equation and is equivalent to a fractional Sobolev norm
H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
, and then use a Gronwall argument to handle the errors introduced due to the lack of a precise conservation.
In
[
11
]
, the error terms are of the form
\mathcal{G}_{h}u
and are thus
smooth
and
linear
in
u
.
In our case of (
1.4
) with
\gamma=0
, the error term is
-i\beta u\mathcal{G}_{h}(|u|^{2})
, which is neither smooth nor linear! In order to keep the multiplicity of
u
-terms in check for the Gronwall argument, we still rely on the smoothing of
\mathcal{G}_{h}
to send more terms to the conserved
L^{2}
space. This also explains the restriction to the
\gamma=0
setting: a lack of smoothing means that we cannot control the multiplicity any longer for the cubic term
i\gamma|u|^{2}u
.

The conserved quantity we consider is motivated by observations and arguments in
[
37
]
, ultimately going back to the fact that there is a Lax pair
(\mathcal{L}_{u;\infty},\mathcal{P}_{u;\infty})
(see Proposition
8.1
) for (
1.6
) such that (
1.6
) is equivalently written as
\partial_{t}u=\mathcal{P}_{u;\infty}u
.
This structure combined with Loewner’s theorem makes the quantities
\langle u,(\mathcal{L}_{u;\infty}+1)^{s}u\rangle
particularly convenient for establishing low-regularity a priori bounds in
H^{s}(\mathbb{T})\cap L^{2}_{+}(\mathbb{T})
.
Outside of the Hardy space,
\mathcal{L}_{u;\infty}
is no longer positive definite, so we replace this by
\langle u,(\mathcal{L}^{2}_{u;\infty}+1)^{\frac{s}{2}}u\rangle
. The ensuing Gronwall argument becomes much more involved than in
[
11
]
. Ultimately, these reasons are the source of the regularity restriction
s\geq\frac{1}{2}
.

Lastly, our final main result concerns the infinite depth limit
h\to\infty
of solutions to (
1.1
) to those of (
1.5
). For simplicity, we state the result when
\gamma=0
and globally-in-time. The local-in-time convergence as
h\to\infty
for any
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
is a consequence of the estimates we proved in the course of establishing Theorem
1.1
. See Section
6
.

Theorem 1.5
.

Fix
s\geq\frac{1}{2}
,
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
,
\gamma=0
, and
r>0
as in Theorem
1.4
. Let
u_{0}\in H^{s}(\mathbb{T})\cap B_{r}(0)
and
\{u_{0,h}\}_{1\leq h<\infty}\subset H^{s}(\mathbb{T})\cap B_{r}(0)
be a net such that
u_{0,h}\to u_{0}
in
H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
as
h\to\infty
. Also, let
u_{\infty}
and
u_{h}
denote the global solutions to (
1.6
) and (
1.1
), respectively, with
u_{\infty}|_{t=0}=u_{0}
and
u_{h}|_{t=0}=u_{0,h}
constructed in Theorem
1.4
. Then,
u_{h}
converges to
u_{\infty}
as
h\to\infty
in
C(\mathbb{R};H^{s}(\mathbb{T}))
.
2
2

2

We endow
C(\mathbb{R};H^{s}(\mathbb{T}))
with the compact-open topology in time.

In particular, when
s>\frac{17}{20}
, the convergence occurs unconditionally: the uniqueness of both the CCM (
1.6
) and INLS (
1.1
) solutions holds in the entire class
C(\mathbb{R};H^{s}(\mathbb{T}))
.

The notion of unconditional convergence property stated in Theorem
1.5
was first introduced in
[
21
]
.
3
3

3

In the context of the intermediate long wave equation converging to BO in the deep-water limit. See
[
44
,
12
]
.
The significance of this is that the convergence happens irrespective of how the approximations
\{u_{h}\}_{1\leq h<\infty}
or the limit
u_{\infty}
were constructed.

1.3.
On the proof of Theorem
1.1

The proof of local well-posedness in Theorem
1.1
follows the strategy in
[
48
,
49
,
50
,
10
]
.
However, we encounter some new difficulties, absent in both
[
50
]
and from our previous work in the line setting
[
10
]
. Moreover, these difficulties are entirely due to working
outside
of the Hardy space and do not appear for CCM in the Hardy space. Combined with our previous work
[
10
]
, this again highlights the difference in behaviour of CCM in and outside of the Hardy space.

Firstly, given a solution
u
to (
1.1
),
we work with the gauged variables
(\widetilde{v},w)
defined in (
1.8
), instead of the pair
(v,w)
where
v(t,x):=\mathbf{P}_{\textup{+,hi}}[e^{i\beta F[u]}u](t,x)
, used on the line
[
10
]
.
The pair
(\widetilde{v},w)
is more suitable for the periodic study, as we clarify in the following.
Examining the equations satisfied by
(v,w)
(see (
3.6
)-(
3.7
)),
we note that there are additional terms on the right-hand side when compared to the Euclidean setting
[
10
, (3.14)-(3.15)]
, due to
F
being the mean-zero primitive of
|u|^{2}
; see (
3.4
).
Some of these are harmless, but
the last linear derivative term in (
3.6
) for
v
,
2\beta\mathbf{P}_{0}(|u|^{2})\partial_{x}v
, creates an obstruction for the well-posedness, as its linearity (in
v
) means that we cannot absorb the derivative loss. However, the mass

\displaystyle M(u):=2\pi\mathbf{P}_{0}(|u|^{2})=\int_{\mathbb{T}}|u|^{2}dx

(1.13)

is conserved for (
1.1
). Consequently, it is possible to remove this bad term through the use of the gauge transform

\displaystyle\widetilde{v}(t,x):=\mathcal{J}_{u}[v](t,x):=v\big(t,x-\tfrac{\beta}{\pi}M(u)t\big),

(1.14)

which explains the presence of the term
\widetilde{v}
in (
1.8
), as a replacement for
v
. As the gauged equations (
3.10
) and (
3.7
) for
(\widetilde{v},w)
are not closed, we are forced to work with the variable quartet
(u,\widetilde{u},\widetilde{v},w)
, where
\widetilde{u}:=\mathcal{J}_{u}[u]
. Although it may seem more natural to work with
(\widetilde{u},\widetilde{v},\widetilde{w})
, this is also not suitable for (
1.1
) on the torus, as we explain below.

In the simpler setting of (
1.1
) with
\gamma=0
and only positive frequencies, namely CCM (
1.6
) in the Hardy space, we have
w\equiv 0
.
It is then possible to apply
\mathcal{J}_{u}
at a “global level”: namely, given a smooth solution
u
to (
1.6
), it suffices to run the local well-posedness argument for
(\widetilde{u},\widetilde{v})
. Here, our arguments yield that the solution map
u_{0}\mapsto\widetilde{u}
is locally Lipschitz continuous in
Z^{s}_{T}
; see (
1.7
).
It is then the inversion of
\mathcal{J}_{u}
that causes the solution map
u_{0}\mapsto u
to be merely continuous.

In the more general case of INLS (
1.1
), or (
1.6
) outside of the Hardy space,

w
no longer vanishes.
One could consider using the gauge
\mathcal{J}_{u}
at a “global level” and work with the trio
(\widetilde{u},\widetilde{v},\widetilde{w})
instead. Unfortunately, while the change of variable
v\mapsto\widetilde{v}
removes the term
2\beta\mathbf{P}_{0}(|u|^{2})\partial_{x}v
from the
v
-equation, the change of variable
w\mapsto\widetilde{w}

introduces
an analogous dangerous term in the
\widetilde{w}
-equation!
Hence, the variable
quartet

(u,\widetilde{u},\widetilde{v},w)
is the appropriate choice for our analysis.

The variable
\widetilde{u}
plays only an auxiliary role in showing the relevant a priori bounds, as our final estimates are only in terms of
(u,\widetilde{v},w)
; see Subsection
5.1
. Also, some estimates for
u
, such as Lemma
3.5
, rely on the recovery formula (
3.13
) which involves
(v,w)
, not
(\widetilde{v},w)
, but this is not problematic as the the
L^{\infty}_{T}H^{s}_{x}
- and
L^{4}_{T}W^{s,4}_{x}
-norms remain invariant under
\mathcal{J}_{u}
. However, the inability to completely remove
\widetilde{u}
from the analysis creates a new difficulty in estimating the
difference
of solutions.

When considering the difference of solutions, we now need to invert the gauge
\mathcal{J}_{u}
at the “local level”, namely, within our construction of low-regularity solutions, and not afterwards. Thus, our difference estimates in Lemma
3.6
become more involved.
Indeed, given two solutions
u_{1},u_{2}
to (
1.1
), we have

\displaystyle\widetilde{v}_{1}-\widetilde{v}_{2}=\mathcal{J}_{u_{1}}[v_{1}]-\mathcal{J}_{u_{2}}[v_{2}]=\mathcal{J}_{u_{1}}[v_{1}-v_{2}]+\mathcal{J}_{u_{1}}[v_{2}]-\mathcal{J}_{u_{2}}[v_{2}],

where the two final terms only differ in the spatial translation parameters depending on the potentially different masses; recall (
1.14
).
In order to see a
genuine difference
u_{1}-u_{2}
to run the bootstrap argument later on (Section
5.3
), we would need to lose derivatives to obtain local Lipschitz continuity of the map
u\mapsto\mathcal{J}_{u}[v]
.
4
4

4

Recall that on the spatial Fourier side,
\mathcal{F}_{x}\{\mathcal{J}_{u}[v]\}(t,\xi)=e^{i\xi\tfrac{\beta}{\pi}M(u)t}\widehat{v}(t,\xi)
.

This is untenable.
Instead, we proceed via frequency truncation, writing

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{u_{1}}[v_{2}]-\mathcal{J}_{u_{2}}[v_{2}]=\Pi_{\leq\Lambda}\big(\mathcal{J}_{u_{1}}[v_{2}]-\mathcal{J}_{u_{2}}[v_{2}]\big)+\Pi_{>\Lambda}\big(\mathcal{J}_{u_{1}}[v_{2}]-\mathcal{J}_{u_{2}}[v_{2}]\big),

where
\Pi_{\leq\Lambda}
is a Fourier projector to frequencies
\{|\xi|\leq\Lambda\}
,
\Lambda\in\mathbb{N}
, and
\Pi_{>\Lambda}=\textup{Id}-\Pi_{\leq\Lambda}
.
For the low-frequency part, we obtain a difference estimate due to the Lipschitz continuity of
\mathcal{J}_{u}
, at the cost of
losing powers of
t\Lambda
.
For the high-frequency part,
we dispense with the need for a genuine difference by proving an equicontinuity-type result for
\Pi_{>\Lambda}\widetilde{v}
in the relevant Fourier restriction norms
X^{s,\frac{1}{2}+}_{T}
; see Proposition
5.3
.

To show this equicontinuity result (Proposition
5.3
), one would hope to exhibit some nonlinear smoothing effect in order to gain negative powers of
\Lambda
. However, this is not possible for the first and last contributions in (
3.10
).
Remarkably, due to the sign structure of the main trilinear term
\Pi_{>\Lambda}\mathbf{P}_{+}[\widetilde{v}\,\mathbf{P}_{-}\partial_{x}(|\widetilde{u}|^{2})]
, the exact same operator
\Pi_{>\Lambda}
can be added to the
\widetilde{v}
factor for free, and the main trilinear estimate (
4.1
) then applies to give a small power of
T
and a factor of
\|\Pi_{>\Lambda}\widetilde{v}\|_{X^{s,\frac{1}{2}+}_{T}}
, which can be “hidden” on the right-hand side of the estimate. The same applies to the last term in (
3.10
), as it is linear in
\widetilde{v}
. This circumvents any need for nonlinear smoothing for these terms.

For the remaining cubic terms in (
3.10
), we either observe
nonlinear smoothing
away from their resonances or recover factors of both
\Pi_{>\Lambda}\widetilde{v}
and
T
; see Lemma
5.5
. Although, nonlinear smoothing effects for the periodic cubic Schrödinger equation are well-known
[
20
, Section 4.1]
, they do not apply here, since the regularity assumptions for
(u,\widetilde{u})
are unconventional.
Indeed, from (
3.14
), we only know that
u,\widetilde{u}\in X^{s-\frac{1}{2}-,\frac{1}{2}}_{T}
rather than
u\in X^{s,\frac{1}{2}}_{T}
, consituting a loss of
\frac{1}{2}
of a derivative.
Interestingly, our setting is more akin to that encountered in the area of quasi-invariance of Gaussian measures under the flow of Hamiltonian PDE
[
61
]
, where one needs energy estimates for a
H^{s}
-functional but with input functions only of regularity
s-\frac{1}{2}-
; see for instance the case of one dimensional periodic cubic NLS in
[
22
]
.

Combining the arguments above, for sufficiently small
T>0
, we obtain differences estimates depending only on the
H^{\frac{1}{2}}
-norms of the initial data
u_{1}(0),u_{2}(0)
:

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{v}_{1}-\widetilde{v}_{2}\|_{X^{\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}+}_{T}}+\|w_{1}-w_{2}\|_{X^{\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}+}_{T}}\leq C(T,\Lambda)\|u_{1}(0)-u_{2}(0)\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}}+\Lambda^{-\theta},

together with similar estimates for the relevant norms of
(u_{j},\widetilde{u}_{j})
,
j=1,2
. By first taking the limit as
u_{1}(0)\to u_{2}(0)
, followed by the limit
\Lambda\to\infty
, we can construct solutions and obtain the continuity of the solution map.
Note that we only recover local Lipschitz continuity on the closed subspace of
H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
comprising functions of a fixed mass.
5
5

5

Similar to the case of the periodic derivative nonlinear Schrodinger equation in
[
30
]
.
This is in contrast to the line setting, where the solution map is locally Lipschitz continuous for any
s>\frac{1}{4}

[
19
,
10
]
and, in fact, smooth when
s>\frac{1}{2}
and the initial data is sufficiently small
[
19
]
. This latter result relies heavily on dispersive smoothing effects on
\mathbb{R}
and is thus not applicable on
\mathbb{T}
.

Lastly, since (
1.1
) is
L^{2}
-critical, it is not enough to restrict to the case of small data, via a scaling argument. We thus need to gain factors of
T
to ensure smallness in the bootstrap estimates.
In the estimates for
(u_{1}-u_{2})
and
(\widetilde{u}_{1}-\widetilde{u}_{2})
in
L^{\infty}_{T}H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
, there is no obvious gain of
T
. As in
[
29
]
, we decompose into high and low frequencies depending on a parameter
M\gg 1
. For the high-frequency contribution, we use the recovery formula (
3.12
). However, for the ensuing difference term
\mathbf{P}_{\gtrsim M}[(e^{-i\beta F[u_{1}]}-e^{-i\beta F[u_{2}]})v_{1}]
, the worst contribution is

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}_{\ll M}\big[e^{-i\beta F[u_{1}]}-e^{-i\beta F[u_{2}]}\big]\mathbf{P}_{\gtrsim M}v_{1}

(1.15)

since, naively, we only obtain smallness from a factor
\|\mathbf{P}_{\gtrsim M}u_{1}(0)\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}}
(after inserting the Duhamel formula for
v_{1}
). Now, if
u_{1}(0)\in H^{s}
for
s>\frac{1}{2}
, then
by Bernstein’s inequality, we may choose
M
large enough depending only on
\|u_{1}(0)\|_{H^{s}}
to make this contribution small (see (
5.54
)), and then choose
T\sim M^{-a}
to make other terms small. Unfortunately, when
s=\frac{1}{2}
, the dependency of
M
, and hence
T
, on
u_{1}(0)
is no longer explicit. While we can still close the local well-posedness argument, it becomes unclear how to globalise solutions when the local time
T
depends on the profile of the data and not just its norm.
The fix we devise is to keep the projection
\mathbf{P}_{\ll M}

on the first term in (
1.15
) and exploit the fact that if this term was evaluated at time
t=0
, no smallness would be required to handle it. By the fundamental theorem of calculus, it turns out that the remaining part can be estimated in
L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}(\mathbb{T})
with a gain of a power of
T
; see Lemma
5.7
. Upon further inspection, this issue at the endpoint
s=\frac{1}{2}
also appears in
[
29
]
for the modified BO equation in the energy space. We expect that our approach here may also be effective in
[
29
]
.

We conclude this introduction with a number of remarks.

Remark 1.6
.

As (
1.1
) is critical in
L^{2}(\mathbb{T})
, the question of local well-posedness in
H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
for
0\leq s<\frac{1}{2}
remains open. An inspection of our trilinear estimates in Proposition
4.1
shows that the worst case is the nearly-resonant one
|\xi|\sim|\xi_{3}|
. In the periodic setting, it is crucial to obtain smoothing through multilinear dispersive effects, captured by the phase function

\displaystyle\Phi(\xi,\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3})=\xi^{2}-\xi_{1}^{2}+\xi_{2}^{2}-\xi_{3}^{2}=2(\xi-\xi_{3})(\xi_{3}-\xi_{2}),

where the second equality holds under the convolution condition
\xi=\xi_{1}-\xi_{2}+\xi_{3}
. In particular,
the precise interaction
\xi=\xi_{3}
is fatal since
\Phi=0
. On the line, in
[
10
]
, we were able to overcome these nearly-resonant interactions by gaining additional smoothing from bilinear Strichartz estimates, which unfortunately, do not hold true on
\mathbb{T}
. It is also not clear to us if these resonant contributions in the nonlinear term
\mathbf{P}_{+}[\widetilde{v}\,\mathbf{P}_{-}\partial_{x}(|\widetilde{v}|^{2})]
could be gauged away as they do not seem to correspond to conservation laws for (
1.1
).

For (
1.1
) with
\gamma=0
on
\mathbb{T}
, another potential approach would be to explore the completely integrable structure developed in
[
55
]
. In fact, it may suffice to leverage these features only for
h=\infty
.
Recently, the third author with L. Gassot
[
26
]
proved well-posedness for dispersive perturbations of BO below
L^{2}(\mathbb{T})
. The first step in this direction would be to construct Birkhoff coordinates for (
1.6
) on
\mathbb{T}
, analogous to the BO setting, which are not yet known, even when restricted to the Hardy space.

Remark 1.7
.

Recently, Kishimoto
[
42
]
proved unconditional uniqueness of the derivative nonlinear Schrödinger equation (DNLS)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}u+i\partial_{x}^{2}u=2\partial_{x}(|u|^{2}u)

(1.16)

in
L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}(\mathbb{T})
.
In view of the nonlinearity in (
1.16
), we see that unconditional uniqueness makes sense whenever
u(t)\in L^{3}(\mathbb{T})
, which is implied by
u(t)\in H^{\frac{1}{6}}(\mathbb{T})
. However, passing to the gauged DNLS variable
v:=e^{-i\partial_{x}^{-1}\mathbf{P}_{\neq 0}(|u|^{2})}u
, which satisfies

\displaystyle\partial_{t}v+i\partial_{x}^{2}v=v^{2}\partial_{x}\overline{v}+\text{lower order terms},

the roughest nonlinear term is
v^{2}\partial_{x}\overline{v}
. Using (
1.9
) and Sobolev embedding, we see that if
u\in L^{\infty}_{T}H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
,
s>\frac{1}{2}
, then this nonlinear term makes sense as a spatial distribution for each fixed time. When
s=\frac{1}{2}
, Kishimoto used refined Strichartz estimates to show that if
u\in L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}(\mathbb{T})
solves (
1.16
) in the sense of distributions, then
u\in L^{4}_{T}L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})
and hence
v\in L^{4}_{T}L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})
. By then integrating in time in the estimate (
1.9
), one can make sense of
v^{2}\partial_{x}\overline{v}
.

To apply this approach to (
1.1
), we need to prove refined Strichartz estimates. A key step here is to establish the estimate

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{P}_{N}[u\mathbf{P}_{+}\partial_{x}(|u|^{2})]\|_{H^{-\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon}_{x}}\leq C_{N}\|u\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}^{3}

(1.17)

for some
\varepsilon>0
, where the constant
C_{N}
may depend on
N\in\mathbb{N}
. Unfortunately, the estimate (
1.17
) is false.
6
6

6

For example, one can take
u
with
\widehat{u}(\xi)=\langle\xi\rangle^{-1}(\log\langle\xi\rangle)^{-\frac{2}{3}}
.

The corresponding estimate for (
1.16
) is

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{P}_{N}\partial_{x}(|u|^{2}u)\|_{H^{-\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon}_{x}}\leq C_{N}\|u\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}^{3},

(1.18)

which does hold true. The main difference between (
1.17
) and (
1.18
) is that the nonlinearity for (
1.1
) cannot be written in divergence form as in (
1.16
). Moreover, (
1.17
) differs from (
1.10
) due to the lack of an extra
\mathbf{P}_{+}
projection on the left-hand side.

Remark 1.8
.

In the recent paper
[
38
]
, the authors investigate the Hamiltonian structure of CCM (
1.6
) in the Hardy space on both
\mathbb{R}
and
\mathbb{T}
. They propose that, based on this structure, the correct model to study on
L^{2}_{+}(\mathbb{T})
is that of the gauged variable
\widetilde{u}
, namely (
3.9
) (when
h=\infty
and
\gamma=0
). We point out that our Theorem
1.1
also applies to (
3.9
).
At a technical level, we also note that simply starting with (
3.9
) instead of (
1.1
) does not solve the difficulties that we encountered in the proof of Theorem
1.1
, since it is not possible to control the corresponding variable
\widetilde{w}=\mathbf{P}_{-,\text{hi}}\widetilde{u}
. One would need to consider
w=\mathcal{J}_{u}^{-1}[\widetilde{w}]
which leads to essentially the same quartet of variables as discussed earlier.

1.4.
Organization of the paper

The remaining of the paper is organised as follows.
In Section
2
, we introduce relevant notation and useful product-type estimates.
Section
3
introduces the gauged variables
v,w
in (
3.5
), establishes the regularity and difference estimates for
(u,\widetilde{u})
.
The crucial trilinear estimates to handle the nonlinearities for
(\widetilde{v},w)
are proven in Section
4
.
In Section
5
we present the proof of the local well-posedness part of Theorem
1.1
. We then prove Theorem
1.5
, the infinite depth limit
h\to\infty
, in Section
6
. In Section
7
, we complete the proof of Theorem
1.1
by establishing the unconditional uniqueness result in items (ii) and (iii).
Lastly, in Section
8
, we consider the global well-posedness results, proving Corollary
1.3
and Theorem
1.4
.

2.
Preliminaries

2.1.
Notation

We use
A\lesssim B
to denote
A\leq CB
for some constant
C>0
,
A\sim B
when
A\lesssim B
and
B\lesssim A
, and
A\ll B
when
A\leq\varepsilon B
for some small
0<\varepsilon<1
.
Our convention for the Fourier transform on
\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{T}
or
\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{R}
is as follows:

\displaystyle(\mathcal{F}f)(\xi)=\widehat{f}(\xi)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{\mathcal{M}}f(x)e^{-ix\xi}dx\quad\text{and}\quad f(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{\widehat{\mathcal{M}}}\widehat{f}(\xi)e^{ix\xi}d\xi,

(2.1)

for
\xi\in\widehat{\mathcal{M}}
, where
\widehat{\mathcal{M}}=\mathbb{Z}
when
\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{T}
and
\widehat{\mathcal{M}}=\mathbb{R}
for
\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{R}
. We will write
\mathcal{F}_{x}
,
\mathcal{F}_{t}
, and
\mathcal{F}_{t,x}
to denote the Fourier transform with respect to the spatial variable, the time variable, or both, respectively, unless it is clear from context.
We then see that

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}_{0}f=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\widehat{f}(0)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}f(x)dx,

(2.2)

and
\mathbf{P}_{\neq 0}=\textup{Id}-\mathbf{P}_{0}
.
We use the following convention for the inner product on
L^{2}(\mathbb{T})
:

\displaystyle\langle f,g\rangle=\int_{\mathbb{T}}\overline{f(x)}g(x)dx.

Next, let
\eta:\mathbb{R}\to[0,1]
be a smooth function with support in
[-2,2]
and equal to
1
on
[-1,1]
. We define
\eta_{N}(\xi)=\eta(\frac{\xi}{N})
and
\psi_{N}(\xi)=\eta(\frac{\xi}{N})-\eta(\frac{2\xi}{N})
.
For
N\in 2^{\mathbb{Z}}
, we denote
the Littlewood-Paley projectors by

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}\,(\mathbf{P}_{\leq N}f)(\xi)=\eta(\tfrac{\xi}{N})\widehat{f}(\xi),\quad\mathcal{F}(\mathbf{P}_{N}f)=\mathcal{F}(\mathbf{P}_{\leq N}f)-\mathcal{F}(\mathbf{P}_{\leq\frac{N}{2}}f)=\eta_{N}\widehat{f},

and
\mathbf{P}_{>N}:=\textup{Id}-\mathbf{P}_{\leq N}
. We then set

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{F}\,(\mathbf{P}_{+}f)(\xi)=\mathbf{1}_{\xi>0}\widehat{f}(\xi),&\quad\mathcal{F}\,(\mathbf{P}_{-}f)(\xi)=\mathbf{1}_{\xi<0}\widehat{f}(\xi),\\
\mathbf{P}_{\textup{lo}}=\mathbf{P}_{\leq 1},\quad\mathbf{P}_{\textup{LO}}=\mathbf{P}_{\leq 10},\quad\mathbf{P}_{\textup{hi}}&=\textup{Id}-\mathbf{P}_{\textup{lo}},\quad\mathbf{P}_{\textup{+,hi}}=\mathbf{P}_{+}\mathbf{P}_{\textup{hi}},\quad\mathbf{P}_{\textup{HI}}=\mathbf{P}_{>10}.\end{split}

We also define two types of frequency projections for functions on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}
with Fourier variables
(\tau,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}
. Given
K\in 2^{\mathbb{N}}
, we set

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{F}_{t,x}\{\mathbf{Q}_{\ll K}u\}(\tau,\xi)&=\eta_{10^{-10}K}(\tau-\xi^{2})\widehat{u}(\tau,\xi),\\
\mathcal{F}_{t,x}\{\mathbf{Q}_{\gtrsim K}u\}(\tau,\xi)&=(1-\eta_{10^{-10}K})(\tau-\xi^{2})\widehat{u}(\tau,\xi).\end{split}

(2.3)

We will use
\Pi_{>\Lambda}
to denote the sharp frequency projection with multiplier
\mathbf{1}_{\{|\xi|>\Lambda\}}
for
\Lambda\in\mathbb{N}
. Similarly, we define
\Pi_{\leq\Lambda}
with multiplier
\mathbf{1}_{\{|\xi|\leq\Lambda\}}
.

For
1<p<\infty
, we define the dyadic
L^{p}
-spaces via the norm

\displaystyle\|f\|_{\widetilde{L^{p}_{t,x}}}=\bigg(\sum_{N\in 2^{\mathbb{N}}}\|\mathbf{P}_{N}f\|_{L^{p}_{t,x}}^{2}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

By the Littlewood-Payley square function theorem and Minkowski’s inequality, it holds that

\displaystyle\|f\|_{L^{p}_{t,x}}\lesssim\|f\|_{\widetilde{L^{p}_{t,x}}}

for any
2\leq p<\infty
. We similarly define the spaces
\widetilde{L^{p}_{x}}
. Given
T>0
and a space
X
, we also write
L^{p}_{T}X=L^{p}([0,T];X)
.

2.2.
Product estimates

In this subsection, we collect some auxiliary product estimates. For a proof of the following lemma, see
[
17
,
27
,
9
]
on
\mathbb{R}
and

[
33
,
7
]
for
\mathbb{T}
.

Lemma 2.1

(Fractional Leibniz rule)
.

Let
s>0
and
1<p_{j},q_{j},r\leq\infty

such that
\frac{1}{r}=\frac{1}{p_{j}}+\frac{1}{q_{j}}
,
j=1,2
.
Then, we have

\displaystyle\|J^{s}(fg)\|_{L^{r}_{x}}\lesssim\|J^{s}f\|_{L^{p_{1}}_{x}}\|g\|_{L^{q_{1}}_{x}}+\|f\|_{L^{p_{2}}_{x}}\|J^{s}g\|_{L^{q_{2}}_{x}}.

We now give a sequence of product estimates. The complicated nature of these estimates is due to the lack of the Sobolev embedding of
H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
into
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})
when
s=\frac{1}{2}
.

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
f,g:\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{C}
.

(i)

Given
s\geq\frac{1}{2}
,
we have

\displaystyle\|f\partial_{x}g\|_{H^{s-1}_{x}}\lesssim\big(\|f\|_{H^{\frac{1}{4}}_{x}}+\|J_{x}^{\frac{1}{2}}f\|_{\widetilde{L^{\frac{4}{3}+}_{x}}}\big)\|J_{x}^{s}g\|_{\widetilde{L^{4}_{x}}}+\|f\|_{H^{s-\frac{1}{4}}_{x}}\|J_{x}^{\frac{1}{2}}g\|_{\widetilde{L^{4}_{x}}}.

(2.4)

In particular, for
u_{1},u_{2},u_{3}:\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{C}
, we have

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{P}_{\pm}(u_{1}u_{2})\partial_{x}u_{3}\|_{H^{s-1}_{x}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|u_{1}\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}}\|u_{2}\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}\|J^{s}_{x}u_{3}\|_{\widetilde{L^{4}_{x}}}

\displaystyle\quad+(\|u_{1}\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}\|u_{2}\|_{H^{s}_{x}}+\|u_{1}\|_{H^{s}_{x}}\|u_{2}\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}})\|J_{x}^{\frac{1}{2}}u_{3}\|_{\widetilde{L^{4}_{x}}}.

(2.5)

(ii)

It holds that

\displaystyle\|f\partial_{x}g\|_{H^{-1}_{x}}\lesssim\|f\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}\|g\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}.

(2.6)

Moreover, given
s>\frac{1}{2}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\|f\partial_{x}g\|_{H^{s-1}_{x}}\lesssim\|f\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}+}_{x}}\|g\|_{H^{s}_{x}}+\|f\|_{H^{s}_{x}}\|g\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}+}_{x}}.

(2.7)

Proof.

We first prove (i).
By duality and dyadic decomposition, it is enough to consider the quantity

\displaystyle\sum_{N,N_{1},N_{2}}N^{s-1}N_{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\mathbf{P}_{N}h\cdot\mathbf{P}_{N_{1}}f\cdot\mathbf{P}_{N_{2}}gdx=:\sum_{N,N_{1},N_{2}}I_{N,N_{1},N_{2}}

(2.8)

where
h\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T})
with
\|h\|_{L^{2}}\leq 1
.
We split the summation in (
2.8
) into three pieces. If
N_{2}\sim N
, then by Cauchy-Schwarz and Bernstein inequalities,

\displaystyle\bigg|\sum_{N\sim N_{2}}\sum_{N_{1}}I_{N,N_{1},N_{2}}\bigg|=\bigg|\sum_{N\sim N_{2}}N^{s}_{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\mathbf{P}_{N}h\cdot f\cdot\mathbf{P}_{N_{2}}gdx\bigg|

\displaystyle\lesssim\|f\|_{L^{4}}\sum_{N\sim N_{2}}N_{2}^{s}\|\mathbf{P}_{N}h\|_{L^{2}}\|\mathbf{P}_{N_{2}}g\|_{L^{4}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|f\|_{H^{\frac{1}{4}}}\|J^{s}g\|_{\widetilde{L^{4}}}.

If
N_{2}\ll N
, then we have
N\sim N_{1}
and hence

\displaystyle\sum_{N\sim N_{1}}\sum_{N_{2}\ll N}|I_{N,N_{1},N_{2}}|

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{N\sim N_{1}}\sum_{N_{2}\ll N}\Big(\frac{N_{2}}{N}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}N_{2}^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\mathbf{P}_{N_{2}}g\|_{L^{4}}N^{-\frac{1}{4}}\|\mathbf{P}_{N}h\|_{L^{4}}N_{1}^{s-\frac{1}{4}}\|\mathbf{P}_{N_{1}}f\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|J^{\frac{1}{2}}g\|_{\widetilde{L^{4}}}\|h\|_{L^{2}}\|f\|_{H^{s-\frac{1}{4}}}.

Lastly, when
N_{2}\gg N
so that
N_{2}\sim N_{1}
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{N_{2}\sim N_{1}}\sum_{N\ll N_{2}}|I_{N,N_{1},N_{2}}|

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{N_{2}\sim N_{1}}\sum_{N\ll N_{2}}N_{2}N^{s-1}\|\mathbf{P}_{N}h\|_{L^{\infty-}}\|\mathbf{P}_{N_{2}}g\|_{L^{4}}\|\mathbf{P}_{N_{1}}f\|_{L^{\frac{4}{3}+}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{N_{2}\sim N_{1}}\|J^{s}\mathbf{P}_{N_{2}}g\|_{L^{4}}\|J^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathbf{P}_{N_{1}}f\|_{L^{\frac{4}{3}+}}\sum_{N\ll N_{2}}\Big(\frac{N}{N_{2}}\Big)^{s-\frac{1}{2}-}\|\mathbf{P}_{N}h\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|J^{s}g\|_{\widetilde{L^{4}}}\|J^{\frac{1}{2}}f\|_{\widetilde{L^{\frac{4}{3}+}}}\|h\|_{L^{2}}.

This completes the proof of (
2.4
).

For (
2.5
), we apply (
2.4
) with
f=\mathbf{P}_{\pm}(u_{1}u_{2})
and
g=u_{3}
. By the fractional Leibniz rule and Sobolev embedding we have

\displaystyle\|u_{1}u_{2}\|_{H^{\frac{1}{4}}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|J^{\frac{1}{4}}u_{1}\|_{L^{2+}}\|u_{2}\|_{L^{\infty-}}+\|u_{1}\|_{L^{\infty-}}\|J^{\frac{1}{4}}u_{2}\|_{L^{2+}}\lesssim\|u_{1}\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}}\|u_{2}\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}},

\displaystyle\|u_{1}u_{2}\|_{H^{s-\frac{1}{4}}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|J^{s-}u_{1}\|_{L^{2+}}\|u_{2}\|_{L^{\infty-}}+\|u_{1}\|_{L^{\infty-}}\|J^{s-}u_{2}\|_{L^{2+}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|u_{1}\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}}\|u_{2}\|_{H^{s}}+\|u_{1}\|_{H^{s}}\|u_{2}\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}}.

Next, by Hölder and Bernstein inequalities, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{N}N\|\mathbf{P}_{\ll N}u_{1}\cdot\widetilde{\mathbf{P}}_{N}u_{2}\|_{L^{\frac{4}{3}+}}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\bigg(\sum_{N}N\|\widetilde{\mathbf{P}}_{N}u_{2}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\bigg)\|u_{1}\|_{H^{\frac{1}{4}+}}^{2}\lesssim\|u_{2}\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}}^{2}\|u_{1}\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}^{2},

\displaystyle\sum_{N}N\|\mathbf{P}_{\gtrsim N}u_{1}\cdot\mathbf{P}_{\gtrsim N}u_{2}]\|_{L^{\frac{4}{3}+}}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{N}N^{-\frac{1}{2}+}\|u_{1}\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}}^{2}\|u_{2}\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}}^{2}\lesssim\|u_{1}\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}}^{2}\|u_{2}\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}}^{2},

where
\widetilde{\mathbf{P}}_{N}
denotes a wider projection.
Then, combining these inequalities with the boundedness of
\mathbf{P}_{\pm}
on
L^{p}
for
1<p<\infty
and

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{P}_{N}[u_{1}u_{2}]\|_{L^{\frac{4}{3}+}}\leq\|\mathbf{P}_{\gtrsim N}u_{1}\cdot\mathbf{P}_{\gtrsim N}u_{2}]\|_{L^{\frac{4}{3}+}}+\|\mathbf{P}_{\ll N}u_{1}\cdot\widetilde{\mathbf{P}}_{N}u_{2}\|_{L^{\frac{4}{3}+}}+\|\widetilde{\mathbf{P}}_{N}u_{1}\cdot\mathbf{P}_{\ll N}u_{2}\|_{L^{\frac{4}{3}+}},

we obtain

\displaystyle\|J^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathbf{P}_{\pm}(u_{1}u_{2})\|_{\widetilde{L^{\frac{4}{3}+}}}\lesssim\|u_{1}\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}}\|u_{2}\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}}.

This completes the proof of (
2.5
).

To show (ii), note that (
2.6
) is implied by the following bound:

\displaystyle\bigg\|\sum_{\xi=\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}}\frac{\langle\xi_{2}\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\langle\xi\rangle\langle\xi_{1}\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}}u(\xi_{1})v(\xi_{2})d\xi_{1}\bigg\|_{\ell^{2}_{\xi}}\lesssim\|u\|_{\ell^{2}}\|v\|_{\ell^{2}}.

(2.9)

If
|\xi_{2}|\gg|\xi_{1}|
, then by Young’s and Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, we have

LHS (
2.9
)

\displaystyle\lesssim\big\|\big(\langle\cdot\rangle^{-1}u)\ast v\big\|_{\ell^{2}}\lesssim\|\langle\cdot\rangle^{-1}u\|_{\ell^{1}}\|v\|_{\ell^{2}}\lesssim\|u\|_{\ell^{2}}\|v\|_{\ell^{2}}.

If instead
|\xi_{2}|\lesssim|\xi_{1}|
, then

\displaystyle\text{LHS \eqref{Hminus1a}}\lesssim\|\langle\xi\rangle^{-1}(u\ast v)\|_{\ell^{2}_{\xi}}\lesssim\|u\ast v\|_{\ell^{\infty}_{\xi}}\lesssim\|u\|_{\ell^{2}}\|v\|_{\ell^{2}}.

The proof of (
2.7
) is similar to that of (
2.6
) and thus we omit it.
This completes the proof. ∎

We now state some estimates concerning products with exponential factors
e^{iF}
for
F
real-valued.
As
e^{iF}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})
, it follows that all Littlewood-Payley type frequency projectors acting on expressions of the form
e^{iF}
are well-defined. In particular, the function
\mathbf{P}_{\textup{lo}}e^{iF}
is supported on frequencies
|\xi|\lesssim 1
. This is in stark contrast to the case on the real-line, where

e^{iF}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})\setminus L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
and more care is needed. See for example
[
50
,
52
,
10
]
.

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
2\leq p<\infty
and assume
F_{1},F_{2}
are two real-valued functions such that

\partial_{x}F_{j}=|f_{j}|^{2}
with
f_{j}\in H^{\frac{1}{2}}(\mathbb{T})
, for
j=1,2
. Then, it holds that

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{P}_{\gtrsim N}e^{iF_{1}}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\lesssim N^{-1+}\|f_{1}\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}^{2},

(2.10)

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{P}_{N}e^{iF_{1}}\|_{L^{p}_{x}}\lesssim N^{-2+}\|f_{1}\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}^{4}+N^{-1+}\|\mathbf{P}_{\gtrsim N}f_{1}\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{p}}_{x}}\|f_{1}\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}},

(2.11)

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{P}_{\gtrsim N}(e^{iF_{1}}-e^{iF_{2}})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}

\displaystyle\lesssim N^{-1+}(\|f_{1}\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}+\|f_{2}\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}})\big\{\|f_{1}-f_{2}\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}+(\|f_{1}\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}+\|f_{2}\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}})\|e^{iF_{1}}-e^{iF_{2}}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\big\},

(2.12)

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{P}_{N}(e^{iF_{1}}-e^{iF_{2}})\|_{L^{p}_{x}}

\displaystyle\lesssim N^{-2+}(\|f_{1}\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}+\|f_{2}\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}})^{3}\big\{\|f_{1}-f_{2}\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}+(\|f_{1}\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}+\|f_{2}\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}})\|e^{iF_{1}}-e^{iF_{2}}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\big\}

\displaystyle\quad+N^{-1+}\big\{\|\mathbf{P}_{\gtrsim N}(f_{1}-f_{2})\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{p}}_{x}}\|f_{1}+f_{2}\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}+\|\mathbf{P}_{\gtrsim N}(f_{1}+f_{2})\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{p}}_{x}}\|f_{1}-f_{2}\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}\big\}

\displaystyle\quad+N^{-1+}\|\mathbf{P}_{\gtrsim N}f_{2}\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{p}}_{x}}\|f_{2}\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}\|e^{iF_{1}}-e^{iF_{2}}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}.

(2.13)

Proof.

By Cauchy-Schwarz, we have

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{P}_{\lesssim N}f\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\lesssim(\log N)^{\frac{1}{2}}\|f\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}.

(2.14)

Then, we get

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\|\mathbf{P}_{\gtrsim N}(fg)\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\\
&\lesssim N^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\mathbf{P}_{\gtrsim N}(fg)\|_{L^{2}_{x}}\\
&\lesssim N^{\frac{1}{2}}\big\{\|\mathbf{P}_{\gtrsim N}f\cdot\mathbf{P}_{\ll N}g\|_{L^{2}_{x}}+\|\mathbf{P}_{\gtrsim N}g\cdot\mathbf{P}_{\ll N}f\|_{L^{2}_{x}}+\|\mathbf{P}_{\gtrsim N}f\cdot\mathbf{P}_{\gtrsim N}g\|_{L^{2}_{x}}\big\}\\
&\lesssim N^{\frac{1}{2}}\big\{\|\mathbf{P}_{\gtrsim N}f\|_{L^{2}_{x}}\|\mathbf{P}_{\ll N}g\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}+\|\mathbf{P}_{\gtrsim N}g\|_{L^{2}_{x}}\|\mathbf{P}_{\ll N}f\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}+\|\mathbf{P}_{\gtrsim N}f\|_{L^{4}_{x}}\|\mathbf{P}_{\gtrsim N}g\|_{L^{4}_{x}}\big\}\\
&\lesssim N^{0+}\|f\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}\|g\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}.\end{split}

(2.15)

It then follows from (
2.15
) that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|\mathbf{P}_{\gtrsim N}e^{iF_{1}}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}&\lesssim N^{-1}\|\mathbf{P}_{\gtrsim N}(\mathbf{P}_{\neq 0}(|f_{1}|^{2})e^{iF_{1}})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\\
&\lesssim N^{-1}\big\{\|\mathbf{P}_{\gtrsim N}[\mathbf{P}_{\neq 0}(|f_{1}|^{2})]\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}+\|\mathbf{P}_{\ll N}\mathbf{P}_{\neq 0}(|f_{1}|^{2})\cdot\mathbf{P}_{\gtrsim N}e^{iF_{1}}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\big\}\\
&\lesssim N^{-1+}\|f_{1}\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}^{2}+N^{-1+}\||f_{1}|^{2}\|_{L^{\infty-}_{x}}\lesssim N^{-1+}\|f_{1}\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}^{2}.\end{split}

(2.16)

This proves (
2.10
).
Therefore, proceeding as in (
2.16
) with (
2.15
), we have

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{P}_{\gtrsim N}[e^{iF_{1}}-e^{iF_{2}}]\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}

\displaystyle\lesssim N^{-1}\big\{\|\mathbf{P}_{\gtrsim N}[\mathbf{P}_{\neq 0}(|f_{1}|^{2}-|f_{2}|^{2})e^{iF_{1}}]\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}+\|\mathbf{P}_{\gtrsim N}[\mathbf{P}_{\neq 0}(|f_{2}|^{2})(e^{iF_{1}}-e^{iF_{2}})]\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\big\}

\displaystyle\lesssim N^{-1}\big\{\|\mathbf{P}_{\gtrsim N}[\mathbf{P}_{\neq 0}(|f_{1}|^{2}-|f_{2}|^{2})]\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}+\|\mathbf{P}_{\ll N}\mathbf{P}_{\neq 0}(|f_{1}|^{2}-|f_{2}|^{2})\cdot\mathbf{P}_{\gtrsim N}e^{iF_{1}}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}+\

\displaystyle\hphantom{X}+\|\mathbf{P}_{\gtrsim N}[\mathbf{P}_{\neq 0}(|f_{2}|^{2})]\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\|e^{iF_{1}}-e^{iF_{2}}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}+\|\mathbf{P}_{\ll N}\mathbf{P}_{\neq 0}(|f_{2}|^{2})\cdot\mathbf{P}_{\gtrsim N}(e^{iF_{1}}-e^{iF_{2}})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\big\}

\displaystyle\lesssim N^{-1+}\|f_{1}+f_{2}\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}\|f_{1}-f_{2}\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}+N^{-1+}\||f_{1}|^{2}-|f_{2}|^{2}\|_{L^{\infty-}_{x}}+N^{-1+}\|f_{2}\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}^{2}\|e^{iF_{1}}-e^{iF_{2}}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}

\displaystyle\lesssim N^{-1+}(\|f_{1}\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}+\|f_{2}\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}})\big\{\|f_{1}-f_{2}\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}+(\|f_{1}\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}+\|f_{2}\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}})\|e^{iF_{1}}-e^{iF_{2}}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\big\},

proving (
2.12
).
Let
2\leq p<\infty
. By Bernstein’s inequality and (
2.14
), we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|\widetilde{\mathbf{P}}_{N}(fg)\|_{L^{p}_{x}}&\lesssim N^{0+}\|\widetilde{\mathbf{P}}_{N}(fg)\|_{L^{p-}_{x}}\\
&\lesssim N^{0+}\big\{\|\mathbf{P}_{\gtrsim N}f\cdot g\|_{L^{p-}_{x}}+\|\mathbf{P}_{\ll N}f\cdot\widetilde{\mathbf{P}}_{N}g\|_{L^{p-}_{x}}\big\}\\
&\lesssim N^{0+}\big\{\|\mathbf{P}_{\gtrsim N}f\|_{L^{p}_{x}}\|g\|_{L^{\infty-}_{x}}+\|f\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}\|\widetilde{\mathbf{P}}_{N}g\|_{L^{p}_{x}}\big\}\\
&\lesssim N^{0+}\big\{\|\mathbf{P}_{\gtrsim N}f\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{p}}_{x}}\|g\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}+\|f\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}\|\mathbf{P}_{\gtrsim N}g\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{p}}_{x}}\big\}.\end{split}

(2.17)

Then, from (
2.15
), (
2.16
), (
2.17
), and (
2.12
), we have

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{P}_{N}(e^{iF_{1}}-e^{iF_{2}})\|_{L^{p}_{x}}

\displaystyle\lesssim N^{-1}\big\{\|\mathbf{P}_{\ll N}(|f_{1}|^{2}-|f_{2}|^{2})\|_{L^{p}_{x}}\|\widetilde{\mathbf{P}}_{N}e^{iF_{1}}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}+\|\widetilde{\mathbf{P}}_{N}(|f_{1}|^{2}-|f_{2}|^{2})\|_{L^{p}_{x}}

\displaystyle\hphantom{XX}+\|\mathbf{P}_{\gg N}(|f_{1}|^{2}-|f_{2}|^{2})\mathbf{P}_{\gg N}e^{iF_{1}}\|_{L^{p}_{x}}+\|\mathbf{P}_{\ll N}(|f_{2}|^{2})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\|\widetilde{\mathbf{P}}_{N}(e^{iF_{1}}-e^{iF_{2}})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}

\displaystyle\hphantom{XX}+\|\widetilde{\mathbf{P}}_{N}(|f_{2}|^{2})\|_{L^{p}_{x}}\|e^{iF_{1}}-e^{iF_{2}}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}+\|\mathbf{P}_{\gg N}(|f_{2}|^{2})\mathbf{P}_{\gg N}(e^{iF_{1}}-e^{iF_{2}})\|_{L^{p}_{x}}\big\}

\displaystyle\lesssim N^{-2+}(\|f_{1}\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}+\|f_{2}\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}})^{3}\|f_{1}-f_{2}\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}

\displaystyle\hphantom{XX}+N^{-1+}\big\{\|\mathbf{P}_{\gtrsim N}(f_{1}-f_{2})\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{p}}_{x}}\|f_{1}+f_{2}\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}+\|\mathbf{P}_{\gtrsim N}(f_{1}+f_{2})\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{p}}_{x}}\|f_{1}-f_{2}\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}\big\}

\displaystyle\hphantom{XX}+N^{-2+}(\|f_{1}\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}+\|f_{2}\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}})^{3}\big\{\|f_{1}-f_{2}\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}+(\|f_{1}\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}+\|f_{2}\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}})\|e^{iF_{1}}-e^{iF_{2}}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\big\}

\displaystyle\hphantom{XX}+N^{-1+}\|\mathbf{P}_{\gtrsim N}f_{2}\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{p}}_{x}}\|f_{2}\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}\|e^{iF_{1}}-e^{iF_{2}}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}

where the slight loss in the powers of
N
can be chosen arbitrarily small. This proves (
2.13
) and similar arguments establish (
2.11
).
∎

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
0\leq s<2
and assume that
F
is a real-valued function such that
\partial_{x}F=|f|^{2}
for
f\in H^{\frac{1}{2}}(\mathbb{T})
. Then, we have

\displaystyle\|J^{s}\mathbf{P}_{\textup{hi}}(e^{iF}g)\|_{L^{2}_{x}}\lesssim\|f\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}^{2}\|g\|_{H^{s}_{x}}+\|f\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}(\|f\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}^{3}+\|f\|_{H^{\max(\frac{1}{2},s-\frac{3}{4})}_{x}})\|g\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}.

(2.18)

Moreover, if
F_{j}
are real-valued functions such that
\partial_{x}F_{j}=|f_{j}|^{2}
,
j=1,2
,
R:=\|f_{1}\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}+\|f_{2}\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}
, and
\sigma=\max(\frac{1}{2},s-\frac{3}{4})
, then it holds that

\displaystyle\|J^{s}\mathbf{P}_{\textup{hi}}([e^{iF_{1}}-e^{iF_{2}}]g)\|_{L^{2}_{x}}

\displaystyle\lesssim R(1+R^{2})\{\|f_{1}-f_{2}\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}+R\|e^{iF_{1}}-e^{iF_{2}}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\big\}\|g\|_{H^{s}_{x}}

(2.19)

\displaystyle\hphantom{X}+\{R\|f_{1}-f_{2}\|_{H^{\sigma}_{x}}+(\|f_{1}\|_{H^{\sigma}_{x}}+\|f_{2}\|_{H^{\sigma}_{x}})(\|f_{1}-f_{2}\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}+R\|e^{iF_{1}}-e^{iF_{2}}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}})\}\|g\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}.

Proof.

We begin with (
2.18
). By dyadic decompositions, we have

\displaystyle\|J^{s}_{x}\mathbf{P}_{\textup{hi}}(e^{iF}g)\|_{L^{2}_{x}}^{2}\lesssim\sum_{N}N^{2s}\bigg(\sum_{N_{1},N_{2}}\|\mathbf{P}_{N_{1}}e^{iF}\cdot\mathbf{P}_{N_{2}}g\|_{L^{2}_{x}}\bigg)^{2}.

We split the above sum into two pieces. When
N_{1}\ll N_{2}
, we use (
2.10
) to bound this contribution by

\displaystyle\sum_{N}N^{2s}\bigg(\sum_{N_{1}\ll N_{2}\sim N}N_{1}^{-1+}\|f\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}^{2}\|\mathbf{P}_{N_{2}}g\|_{L^{2}_{x}}\bigg)^{2}\lesssim\|f\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}^{4}\|g\|_{H^{s}_{x}}^{2}.

For the contribution from
N_{1}\gtrsim N_{2}
, we use (
2.14
) and (
2.11
) to obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{N}

\displaystyle N^{2s}\bigg(\sum_{N_{1}\gtrsim N_{2}}\|\mathbf{P}_{N_{1}}e^{iF}\|_{L^{2}_{x}}\|\mathbf{P}_{N_{2}}g\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\bigg)^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{N}N^{2s}\bigg(\sum_{N_{1}}\big\{N_{1}^{-2+}\|f\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}^{4}+N_{1}^{-1+}\|\mathbf{P}_{\gtrsim N_{1}}f\|_{L^{2}_{x}}\|f\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}\big\}\sum_{N_{2}\lesssim N_{1}}N_{2}^{0+}\|g\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}\bigg)^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|f\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}^{8}\|g\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}^{2}+\|f\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}^{2}\|g\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}^{2}\sum_{N}N^{2s}\bigg(\sum_{N_{1}\gtrsim N}N_{1}^{-1+}\|\mathbf{P}_{\gtrsim N_{1}}f\|_{L^{2}_{x}}\bigg)^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|f\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}^{8}\|g\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}^{2}+\|f\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}^{2}\|f\|^{2}_{H^{\max(\frac{1}{2},s-\frac{3}{4})}_{x}}\|g\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}^{2},

completing the proof of (
2.18
). The difference estimate in (
2.19
) follows analogously. ∎

3.
The gauge transform and properties of solutions

3.1.
Fourier restriction norm spaces

For
s,b\in\mathbb{R}
, we consider the Fourier restriction norm spaces
X^{s,b}=X^{s,b}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T})
which are the completion of
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T})
under the norm:

\displaystyle\|u\|_{X^{s,b}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T})}

\displaystyle=\big\|\langle\tau-\xi^{2}\rangle^{b}\langle\xi\rangle^{s}\widehat{u}(\tau,\xi)\big\|_{L^{2}_{\xi}(\mathbb{Z})L^{2}_{\tau}(\mathbb{R})}.

We will use the notation
\sigma=\sigma(\tau,\xi):=\tau-\xi^{2}
.
Given a time interval
I\subset\mathbb{R}
, we define localised in time versions of these spaces as follows: if
u:I\times\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{C}
, then

\displaystyle\|u\|_{X^{s,b}_{I}}:=\inf\{\|z\|_{X^{s,b}}\,:\,z:\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{C},\,\,z|_{I\times\mathbb{T}}=u\}.

When
I=[0,T]
for some
T>0
, we denote the spaces
X^{s,b}_{I}=X^{s,b}_{T}
.
For the time localised spaces, the following embedding holds:

\displaystyle X_{T}^{s,b}\subset C([0,T];H^{s}(\mathbb{T}))

(3.1)

for any
b>\frac{1}{2}
.
We recall the following linear estimates related to the
X^{s,b}
-spaces
[
8
,
59
]
.

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
s,b\in\mathbb{R}
,
\eta
denote a smooth time cutoff, and
S(t)=e^{it\partial_{x}^{2}}
.

(i)
Let
0<\delta<\frac{1}{2}
. Then,

\displaystyle\|\eta(t)S(t)f\|_{X^{s,b}}\lesssim\|f\|_{H^{s}}\quad\text{and}\quad\bigg\|\eta(t)\int_{0}^{t}S(t-t^{\prime})g(t^{\prime})dt^{\prime}\bigg\|_{X^{s,\frac{1}{2}+\delta}}\lesssim\|g\|_{X^{s,-\frac{1}{2}+\delta}}.

(ii)
For any
T>0
and
-\frac{1}{2}<b^{\prime}\leq b<\frac{1}{2}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\|u\|_{X^{s,b^{\prime}}_{T}}\lesssim T^{b-b^{\prime}}\|u\|_{X^{s,b}_{T}}.

(iii)
It holds that

\displaystyle\|u\|_{L^{4}_{t,x}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T})}\lesssim\|u\|_{X^{0,\frac{3}{8}}}\quad\text{and}\quad\|u\|_{L^{4}_{T,x}}\lesssim T^{\frac{1}{8}}\|u\|_{X^{0,\frac{1}{2}}_{T}}.

(3.2)

Finally, we have the following useful
L^{p}
-boundedness result related to the modulation operators
\mathbf{Q}_{\ll K}
. See
[
29
, Lemma 4.6]
.

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
1<p<\infty
,
N,K\in 2^{\mathbb{N}}
satisfying
K\gg N^{2}
, and
\mathbf{Q}_{\ll K}
as in (
2.3
). Then, there exists
C_{p}>0
such that

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{P}_{N}\mathbf{Q}_{\ll K}f\|_{L^{p}_{t,x}}\leq C_{p}\|\mathbf{P}_{N}f\|_{L^{p}_{t,x}}.

(3.3)

3.2.
Gauge transform

We construct a gauge transform as in
[
19
]
.
For a function
f:\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{C}
such that
\mathbf{P}_{0}f=0
, we define its periodic mean zero primitive:

\displaystyle(\partial_{x}^{-1}f)(x):=\mathcal{F}^{-1}[(i\xi)^{-1}\mathcal{F}f](x)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\bigg(\int_{z}^{x}f(y)dy\bigg)dz.

Note that
\partial_{x}^{-1}\partial_{x}=\partial_{x}\partial_{x}^{-1}\mathbf{P}_{\neq 0}=\mathbf{P}_{\neq 0}
.
We then set

\displaystyle F=F[u]=\partial_{x}^{-1}\mathbf{P}_{\neq 0}(|u|^{2})

(3.4)

and thus
\partial_{x}F=\mathbf{P}_{\neq 0}(|u|^{2}).

We then define the gauged variables

\displaystyle v:=\mathbf{P}_{+,\text{hi}}[e^{i\beta F[u]}u]\quad\text{and}\quad w:=\mathbf{P}_{-,\text{hi}}u.

(3.5)

The choice of adding a
\mathbf{P}_{\textup{hi}}
operator to the terms in (
3.5
) is convenient as we cannot assume a mean zero condition for solutions to (
1.1
).
The following lemma determines the dynamics for
(v,w)
in (
3.5
).
We need to introduce further gauged variables
\widetilde{u},\widetilde{v},\widetilde{w}
, to remove a problematic linear term in the equation for
v
defined in (
3.5
).

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
u\in C([0,T];H^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}))
be a solution of (
1.4
) and
v,w
be the gauged variables defined in (
3.5
).

(i)
The gauged variables
v
and
w
defined in (
3.5
) satisfy the following equations:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}v+i\partial_{x}^{2}v

\displaystyle=-2\beta\mathbf{P}_{\textup{+,hi}}[v\mathbf{P}_{-}\partial_{x}(|u|^{2})]+i\gamma\mathbf{P}_{\textup{+,hi}}[e^{i\beta F}|u|^{2}u]-i\beta\mathbf{P}_{\textup{+,hi}}[e^{i\beta F}u\mathcal{G}_{h}(|u|^{2})]

\displaystyle\hphantom{X}+i\tfrac{\beta}{\pi}\big[\tfrac{\beta}{4\pi}M(u)^{2}-P(u)\big]v+\tfrac{\beta}{\pi}M(u)\partial_{x}v,

(3.6)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}w+i\partial_{x}^{2}w

\displaystyle=2\beta\mathbf{P}_{-,\textup{hi}}[w\mathbf{P}_{+}\partial_{x}(|u|^{2})]-i\beta\mathbf{P}_{-,\textup{hi}}[u\mathcal{G}_{h}(|u|^{2})]+i\gamma\mathbf{P}_{-,\textup{hi}}[|u|^{2}u]=:\mathcal{N}_{w}(u),

(3.7)

where
\mathcal{G}_{h}
is as in (
1.3
), and
M(u)
and
P(u)
denote the mass and momentum, as in (
1.13
) and (
1.12
).

(ii)

We define new gauged variables

\displaystyle\begin{split}\widetilde{v}&:=\mathcal{J}_{u}[v],\quad\widetilde{w}:=\mathcal{J}_{u}[w],\\
\widetilde{u}&:=\mathcal{J}_{u}[u],\quad\widetilde{F}:=\mathcal{J}_{u}[F]=F[\widetilde{u}]=\partial_{x}^{-1}\mathbf{P}_{\neq 0}(|\widetilde{u}|^{2}),\end{split}

(3.8)

where
\mathcal{J}_{u}
is as in (
1.14
).
Then,
\widetilde{u},\widetilde{v}
satisfy:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\widetilde{u}+i\partial_{x}^{2}\widetilde{u}

\displaystyle=2\beta\widetilde{u}\mathbf{P}_{+}\partial_{x}(|\widetilde{u}|^{2})-i\beta\widetilde{u}\mathcal{G}_{h}(|\widetilde{u}|^{2})+i\gamma|\widetilde{u}|^{2}\widetilde{u}-\tfrac{\beta}{\pi}M(\widetilde{u})\partial_{x}\widetilde{u},

(3.9)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\widetilde{v}+i\partial_{x}^{2}\widetilde{v}

\displaystyle=-2\beta\mathbf{P}_{\textup{+,hi}}[\widetilde{v}\mathbf{P}_{-}\partial_{x}(|\widetilde{u}|^{2})]+i\gamma\mathbf{P}_{\textup{+,hi}}[e^{i\beta\widetilde{F}}|\widetilde{u}|^{2}\widetilde{u}]

\displaystyle\qquad-i\beta\mathbf{P}_{\textup{+,hi}}[e^{i\beta\widetilde{F}}\widetilde{u}\mathcal{G}_{h}(|\widetilde{u}|^{2})]+i\tfrac{\beta}{\pi}\big[\tfrac{\beta}{4\pi}M(\widetilde{u})^{2}-P(\widetilde{u})\big]\widetilde{v}=:\mathcal{N}_{\widetilde{v}}(\widetilde{u}).

(3.10)

Proof.

Part (ii) follows from (
3.6
)-(
3.7
), the definition of the gauged variables (
3.8
) and the conservation of mass
M(u)
for solutions
u
to (
1.4
). Thus, it suffices to show Part (i), which follows from a modification of the proof in
[
19
, Lemma 3.4]
, with more terms appearing here due to the zeroth frequency.
We only detail the changes needed in the proof of
[
10
, Lemma 3.5]
in the periodic setting.

Using (
1.4
), the fact that
\beta,\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
, and that
\mathcal{G}_{h}
preserves real-valuedness, we have

\displaystyle\partial_{t}F

\displaystyle=\mathbf{P}_{\neq 0}[i(u\partial_{x}\overline{u}-\overline{u}\partial_{x}u)+\beta|u|^{4}]=i(u\partial_{x}\overline{u}-\overline{u}\partial_{x}u)+\beta|u|^{4}-\tfrac{1}{\pi}P(u)

(3.11)

where
P(u)
denotes the momentum in (
1.12
),
and hence

\displaystyle\partial_{t}F+i\partial_{x}^{2}F-\beta(\partial_{x}F)^{2}

\displaystyle=2iu\partial_{x}\overline{u}+\tfrac{\beta}{\pi}M(u)|u|^{2}-\tfrac{\beta}{4\pi^{2}}M(u)^{2}-\tfrac{1}{\pi}P(u).

Combining the above with
[
10
, (3.16)]
and the subsequent simplifications, gives

\displaystyle\partial_{t}v+i\partial_{x}^{2}v

\displaystyle=-2\beta\mathbf{P}_{+,\textup{hi}}[v\mathbf{P}_{-}\partial_{x}(|u|^{2})]+i\gamma\mathbf{P}_{+,\textup{hi}}[e^{i\beta F}|u|^{2}u]-i\beta\mathbf{P}_{+,\textup{hi}}[e^{i\beta F}u\mathcal{G}_{h}(|u|^{2})]

\displaystyle\quad+i\tfrac{\beta}{\pi}\big[\tfrac{\beta}{4\pi}M(u)^{2}-P(u)\big]v+\tfrac{\beta}{\pi}M(u)\partial_{x}v,

from which (
3.6
) follows. The assertion for
\widetilde{v}
in (
3.10
) follows from the conservation of mass and momentum for
u
,
M(u(t))=M(u_{0})
and
P(u(t))=P(u_{0})
, and direct computation.

To obtain (
3.7
), we simply apply
\mathbf{P}_{-,\text{hi}}
to both sides of (
1.4
) together with
\mathbf{P}_{-,\text{hi}}[u\mathbf{P}_{+}\partial_{x}(|u|^{2})]=\mathbf{P}_{-,\text{hi}}[w\mathbf{P}_{+}\partial_{x}(|u|^{2})]
,
completing the proof.
∎

Remark 3.4
.

The transformation
\mathcal{J}_{u}
in (
1.14
) and the mapping
v\mapsto\widetilde{v}
explicitly exploit the conservation of mass
M(u)
as in (
1.13
), to remove the problematic
\partial_{x}v
-term in (
3.6
).
Note that (
3.10
) still contains a linear
\widetilde{v}
-term, which could also be removed by considering
\widetilde{v}\mapsto e^{-i\frac{\beta}{\pi}[\frac{\beta}{4\pi}M(\widetilde{u})^{2}-P(\widetilde{u})]t}\widetilde{v}
, taking advantage of the conservation of both mass
M(\widetilde{u})
and momentum
P(\widetilde{u})
in (
1.13
) and (
1.12
).
However, we choose to not incorporate this additional phase rotation and work with
\widetilde{v}
instead, as this linear term is harmless for the well-posedness analysis.
Moreover,
this allows us to simplify the unconditional uniqueness argument in Section
7
, as we only need to justify conservation of mass
M(u)
(see (
7.6
), for example) for
u\in L^{\infty}_{T}H^{s}_{x}\cap L^{4}_{T}W^{s,4}_{x}
, but not the conservation of momentum
P(u)
.

3.3.
Properties of the gauged variables

We have the following recovery result for the solution
u
to (
1.1
) depending on the gauged variables
v,w
in (
3.5
):

\displaystyle u

\displaystyle=\mathbf{P}_{\textup{+,hi}}u+w+\mathbf{P}_{\textup{lo}}u

\displaystyle=\mathbf{P}_{\textup{+,hi}}[e^{-i\beta F}v]+\mathbf{P}_{\textup{+,hi}}[e^{-i\beta F}\mathbf{P}_{+,\text{lo}}(e^{i\beta F}u)]+\mathbf{P}_{\textup{+,hi}}(e^{-i\beta F})\mathbf{P}_{0}(e^{i\beta F}u)

\displaystyle\hphantom{X}+\mathbf{P}_{\textup{+,hi}}[\mathbf{P}_{+}(e^{-i\beta F})\mathbf{P}_{-}(e^{i\beta F}u)]+w+\mathbf{P}_{\textup{lo}}u.

(3.12)

Applying
\mathbf{P}_{\text{HI}}
to both sides, we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbf{P}_{\textup{HI}}u&=\mathbf{P}_{\textup{+,HI}}[e^{-i\beta F}v]+\mathbf{P}_{\textup{+,HI}}[\mathbf{P}_{\textup{hi}}(e^{-i\beta F})\mathbf{P}_{+,\text{lo}}(e^{i\beta F}u)]\\
&\hphantom{X}+\mathbf{P}_{\textup{+,HI}}(e^{-i\beta F})\mathbf{P}_{0}(e^{i\beta F}u)+\mathbf{P}_{\textup{+,HI}}[\mathbf{P}_{+}(e^{-i\beta F})\mathbf{P}_{-}(e^{i\beta F}u)]+\mathbf{P}_{\textup{HI}}w.\end{split}

(3.13)

Note that in the second term, the projectors
\mathbf{P}_{\textup{HI}}
and
\mathbf{P}_{\textup{lo}}
allow us to place for free an extra projector
\mathbf{P}_{\textup{hi}}
onto the first factor
e^{-i\beta F}
.
Moreover, the recovery formulas in (
3.12
) and (
3.13
) also hold for the gauged variable
\widetilde{u}
via applying the translation operator
\mathcal{T}_{u}
in (
1.14
), replacing every instance of
u,v,w,F
with
\widetilde{u},\widetilde{v},\widetilde{w},\widetilde{F}
as in (
3.8
), respectively.

In the following, we obtain estimates for
u
(resp.
\widetilde{u}
) in terms of the gauged variables
v
and
w
in (
3.5
) (resp.
\widetilde{v}
and
\widetilde{w}
in (
3.8
)). To get estimates with small constants, we need to refine the estimate for the
L^{\infty}_{T}H^{s}_{x}
-norm in (
3.16
), and we employ an idea from
[
29
, Proposition 3.2]
.

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
\frac{1}{2}\leq s<2
,
0\leq T\leq 1
,
u
be a solution to (
1.4
) on
[0,T]
,

v,w
be the gauged variables in (
3.5
),
and

\widetilde{u},\widetilde{v},\widetilde{w}
be as in (
3.8
).

(i)
The following estimate holds:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sup_{0\leq\theta\leq 1}\|u\|_{X^{s-\theta,\theta}_{T}}+\sup_{0\leq\theta\leq 1}\|\widetilde{u}\|_{X^{s-\theta,\theta}_{T}}&\lesssim T^{\frac{1}{4}}(1+\|\mathcal{G}_{h}\|_{\textup{op}})\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}^{2}\|J^{s}_{x}u\|_{\widetilde{L^{4}_{T,x}}}\\
&\quad+T^{\frac{1}{4}}\|J_{x}^{\frac{1}{2}}u\|_{L^{4}_{T,x}}\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{s}_{x}}+\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{s}_{x}}.\end{split}

(3.14)

(ii)

Given
\theta>0
and
M\in\mathbb{N}
sufficiently large, we define the quantities

\displaystyle G_{1}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})

\displaystyle:=T^{\frac{1}{8}}(1+\|\mathbf{u}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}^{2}+\|\mathbf{u}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}\|\mathbf{u}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\max(\frac{1}{2},s-\frac{3}{4}+)}_{x}})\|\mathbf{v}\|_{X^{s,\frac{1}{2}}_{T}}

\displaystyle\quad+T^{\frac{1}{4}}(1+\|\mathbf{u}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}^{5})\|\mathbf{u}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}^{2}\|\mathbf{u}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\max(\frac{1}{2},s-\frac{3}{4}+)}_{x}}+T^{\frac{1}{8}}\|\mathbf{w}\|_{X^{s,\frac{1}{2}}_{T}},

\displaystyle G_{2}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})

\displaystyle:=\|\mathbf{u}(0)\|_{H^{s}}+\|\mathbf{v}\|_{X^{s,\frac{1}{2}+}_{T}}+\|\mathbf{w}\|_{X^{s,\frac{1}{2}+}_{T}}

\displaystyle\qquad+T^{\frac{3}{4}}M^{3}(1+\|\mathcal{G}_{h}\|_{\textup{op}})\|\mathbf{u}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}^{2}(\|\mathbf{u}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}+\|J_{x}^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathbf{u}\|_{\widetilde{L^{4}_{T,x}}})

\displaystyle\qquad+M^{-\theta}(1+\|\mathbf{u}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}^{4})(1+\|\mathbf{v}\|_{X^{s,\frac{1}{2}+}_{T}})\|\mathbf{u}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\max(\frac{1}{2},s-\frac{3}{4}+)}_{x}}.

Then, the following estimates hold

\displaystyle\|J_{x}^{s}\mathbf{P}_{\textup{HI}}u\|_{\widetilde{L^{4}_{T,x}}}

\displaystyle\lesssim G_{1}(u,\widetilde{v},w),

\displaystyle\|J_{x}^{s}\mathbf{P}_{\textup{HI}}\widetilde{u}\|_{\widetilde{L^{4}_{T,x}}}

\displaystyle\lesssim G_{1}(\widetilde{u},\widetilde{v},w),

(3.15)

\displaystyle\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{s}_{x}}

\displaystyle\lesssim G_{2}(u,\widetilde{v},w),

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{u}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{s}_{x}}

\displaystyle\lesssim G_{2}(\widetilde{u},\widetilde{v},w).

(3.16)

Proof.

We first prove (
3.14
) in (i) by arguing as in
[
50
, Proposition 3.2]
.
The main point is that we have

\displaystyle\sup_{0\leq\theta\leq 1}\|u\|_{X_{T}^{s-\theta,\theta}}\lesssim\|\partial_{t}u+i\partial_{x}^{2}u\|_{L^{2}_{T}H^{s-1}_{x}}+\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{s}_{x}}.

(3.17)

To estimate the first contribution above, we consider the equation for
u
(
1.4
). Let
\mathcal{Q}_{h}\in\{\text{Id},\mathcal{G}_{h}\}
.
When
s\leq 1
, Sobolev embedding and the fractional Leibniz rule give

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|\mathcal{Q}_{h}(|u|^{2})u\|_{L^{2}_{T}H^{s-1}_{x}}\leq\|\mathcal{Q}_{h}(|u|^{2})u\|_{L^{2}_{T,x}}&\lesssim T^{\frac{1}{4}}\|\mathcal{Q}_{h}(|u|^{2})\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}L^{4}_{x}}\|u\|_{L^{4}_{T,x}}\\
&\lesssim T^{\frac{1}{4}}\|\mathcal{Q}_{h}\|_{\text{op}}\|J_{x}^{\frac{1}{4}}(|u|^{2})\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}L^{2}_{x}}\|u\|_{L^{4}_{T,x}}\\
&\lesssim T^{\frac{1}{4}}\|\mathcal{Q}_{h}\|_{\text{op}}\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{3}{8}}_{x}}^{2}\|u\|_{L^{4}_{T,x}}.\end{split}

(3.18)

When
s\in(1,2)
, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|\mathcal{Q}_{h}(|u|^{2})u\|_{L^{2}_{T}H^{s-1}_{x}}&\lesssim\|\mathcal{Q}_{h}(|u|^{2})u\|_{L^{2}_{T,x}}+\|\mathcal{Q}_{h}(|u|^{2})\partial_{x}u\|_{L^{2}_{T,x}}+\|\mathcal{Q}_{h}\partial_{x}(|u|^{2})u\|_{L^{2}_{T}L^{2-}_{x}}\\
&\lesssim T^{\frac{1}{4}}\|\mathcal{Q}_{h}(|u|^{2})\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}L^{4}_{x}}\|J_{x}u\|_{L^{4}_{T,x}}+\|\mathcal{Q}_{h}\partial_{x}(|u|^{2})\|_{L^{2}_{T,x}}\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}L^{\infty-}_{x}}\\
&\lesssim T^{\frac{1}{4}}\|\mathcal{Q}_{h}\|_{\text{op}}\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}^{2}\|J_{x}^{s}u\|_{L^{4}_{T,x}}.\end{split}

(3.19)

By (
2.5
), Minkowski’s and Hölder’s inequality, we have

\displaystyle\|2\beta u\mathbf{P}_{+}\partial_{x}(|u|^{2})\|_{L^{2}_{T}H^{s-1}_{x}}\lesssim T^{\frac{1}{4}}\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}^{2}\|J^{s}_{x}u\|_{\widetilde{L^{4}_{T,x}}}+T^{\frac{1}{4}}\|J^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}u\|_{L^{4}_{T,x}}\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{s}_{x}}.

Combining the estimates above, we obtain (
3.17
)
for
u
.
The bound for
\widetilde{u}
follows from (
3.17
) and using the equation (
3.9
) for the first term. Comparing (
1.1
) and (
3.9
), there is only one additional term
-\frac{\beta}{\pi}M(\widetilde{u})\partial_{x}\widetilde{u}
, whose
L^{2}_{T}H^{s-1}_{x}
-norm is controlled by the first term in (
3.14
).
This completes the proof of (
3.14
).

We now prove the first estimate in (
3.15
) in (ii).
First, we prove some auxiliary estimates on
v
,
w
, and
|u|^{2}
. By (
3.2
) with
(
3.8
), we have

\displaystyle\|J_{x}^{s}v\|_{\widetilde{L^{4}_{T,x}}}=\|J_{x}^{s}\widetilde{v}\|_{\widetilde{L^{4}_{T,x}}}\lesssim T^{\frac{1}{8}}\|\widetilde{v}\|_{X^{s,\frac{1}{2}}_{T}}\quad\text{and}\quad\|J_{x}^{s}w\|_{\widetilde{L^{4}_{T,x}}}=\|J_{x}^{s}\widetilde{w}\|_{\widetilde{L^{4}_{T,x}}}\lesssim T^{\frac{1}{8}}\|w\|_{X^{s,\frac{1}{2}}_{T}}.

(3.20)

By the recovery formula in (
3.13
) and the triangle inequality, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|\mathbf{P}_{N}\mathbf{P}_{\textup{HI}}u\|_{L^{4}_{T,x}}&\lesssim\|\mathbf{P}_{N}\mathbf{P}_{\textup{+,HI}}[e^{-i\beta F}v]\|_{L^{4}_{T,x}}+\|\mathbf{P}_{N}\mathbf{P}_{\textup{+,HI}}[\mathbf{P}_{\textup{hi}}(e^{-i\beta F})\mathbf{P}_{+,\text{lo}}(e^{i\beta F}u)]\|_{L^{4}_{T,x}}\\
&\hphantom{XX}+\|\mathbf{P}_{0}(e^{i\beta F}u)\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}}\|\mathbf{P}_{N}\mathbf{P}_{\textup{+,HI}}(e^{-i\beta F})\|_{L^{4}_{T,x}}\\
&\hphantom{XX}+\|\mathbf{P}_{N}\mathbf{P}_{\textup{+,HI}}[\mathbf{P}_{+}(e^{-i\beta F})\mathbf{P}_{-}(e^{i\beta F}u)]\|_{L^{4}_{T,x}}+\|\mathbf{P}_{N}\mathbf{P}_{\textup{HI}}w\|_{L^{4}_{T,x}}\\
&=:\hskip 1.42262pt\text{I}\hskip 1.42262pt_{N}+\text{I \hskip-7.96677pt I}_{N}+\text{I \hskip-8.25128pt I \hskip-8.25128pt I}_{N}+\text{I \hskip-7.96677pt V}_{N}+\|\mathbf{P}_{N}\mathbf{P}_{\textup{HI}}w\|_{L^{4}_{T,x}}\end{split}

(3.21)

and we need to estimate each of these terms.
For the first contribution in (
3.21
), we have

\displaystyle\hskip 1.42262pt\text{I}\hskip 1.42262pt_{N}\sim\|\mathbf{P}_{N}[e^{-i\beta F}v]\|_{L^{4}_{T,x}}\lesssim\|\mathbf{P}_{\ll N}(e^{-i\beta F})\widetilde{\mathbf{P}}_{N}v\|_{L^{4}_{T,x}}+\|\mathbf{P}_{N}\big[\mathbf{P}_{\gtrsim N}(e^{-i\beta F})v\big]\|_{L^{4}_{T,x}}.

(3.22)

Then, summing the first term in (
3.22
), we get

\displaystyle\sum_{N}N^{2s}\|\mathbf{P}_{\ll N}(e^{-i\beta F})\widetilde{\mathbf{P}}_{N}v\|_{L^{4}_{T,x}}^{2}\lesssim\|J^{s}_{x}v\|^{2}_{\widetilde{L^{4}_{T,x}}}.

For the second term in (
3.22
), by
(
2.10
), we have

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{P}_{N}[\mathbf{P}_{\gtrsim N}(e^{-i\beta F})v]\|_{L^{4}_{T,x}}\lesssim\|\mathbf{P}_{\gtrsim N}(e^{-i\beta F})\|_{L^{\infty}_{T,x}}\|v\|_{L^{4}_{T,x}}\lesssim N^{-1+}\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}^{2}\|J_{x}^{\frac{1}{2}}v\|_{L^{4}_{T,x}},

so that if
s<1
, then

\displaystyle\sum_{N}N^{2s}\|\mathbf{P}_{N}[\mathbf{P}_{\gtrsim N}(e^{-i\beta F})v]\|_{L^{4}_{T,x}}^{2}\lesssim\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}^{4}\|J_{x}^{\frac{1}{2}}v\|_{L^{4}_{T,x}}^{2}.

When
1\leq s<2
, using (
2.10
), Hölder’s inequality, and Sobolev embedding, we obtain

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{P}_{N}\big[\mathbf{P}_{\gtrsim N}(e^{-i\beta F})v\big]\|_{L^{4}_{T,x}}

\displaystyle\lesssim N^{-1}\|\partial_{x}\mathbf{P}_{N}\big[\mathbf{P}_{\gtrsim N}(e^{-i\beta F})v\big]\|_{L^{4}_{T,x}}

\displaystyle\lesssim N^{-1}\Big(\|\mathbf{P}_{\gg N}(e^{-i\beta F})\|_{L^{\infty}_{T,x}}\|\mathbf{P}_{\neq 0}(|u|^{2})v\|_{L^{4}_{T,x}}+\|\mathbf{P}_{\gtrsim N}u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}L^{4+}_{x}}\|uv\|_{L^{4}_{T}L^{\infty-}_{x}}

\displaystyle\hphantom{XXXXXX}+\|\mathbf{P}_{\gtrsim N}(e^{-i\beta F})\|_{L^{\infty}_{T,x}}\|\partial_{x}v\|_{L^{4}_{T,x}}\Big)

\displaystyle\lesssim N^{-2+}(1+\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}^{2})\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}^{2}\|J_{x}v\|_{L^{4}_{T,x}}

\displaystyle\hphantom{XXX}+N^{-\frac{3}{4}-\max(\frac{1}{2},s-\frac{3}{4}+)}\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\max(\frac{1}{2},s-\frac{3}{4}+)}_{x}}\|J^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}v\|_{L^{4}_{T,x}}.

Therefore, combining the estimates above with and summing in
N
, gives

\displaystyle\bigg(\sum_{N}N^{2s}\hskip 1.42262pt\text{I}\hskip 1.42262pt_{N}^{2}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\lesssim T^{\frac{1}{8}}\big(1+\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}^{2}+\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\max(\frac{1}{2},s-\frac{3}{4}+)}_{x}}\big)\|J^{s}_{x}v\|_{\widetilde{L^{4}_{T,x}}}.

(3.23)

Considering the third contribution in (
3.21
),
we estimate the first factor
by (
2.2
) and Cauchy-Schwarz:

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{P}_{0}(e^{i\beta F}u)\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}L^{2}_{x}}.

(3.24)

For the second factor,
if
s<1
, using (
2.10
), we have

\displaystyle\bigg(\sum_{N}N^{2s}\|\mathbf{P}_{N}\mathbf{P}_{+,\textup{HI}}(e^{-i\beta F})\|_{L^{4}_{T,x}}^{2}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\lesssim T^{\frac{1}{4}}\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}^{2},

(3.25)

while for
1\leq s\leq 2
, using (
2.11
) with
p=4
, we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}\bigg(\sum_{N}N^{2s}\|\mathbf{P}_{N}\mathbf{P}_{+,\textup{HI}}(e^{-i\beta F})\|_{L^{4}_{T,x}}^{2}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}&\lesssim\bigg(\sum_{N}N^{2s-4+}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}T^{\frac{1}{4}}\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}^{4}\\
&\hphantom{X}+T^{\frac{1}{4}}\bigg(\sum_{N}N^{2s-2+}\|J_{x}^{\frac{1}{4}}\mathbf{P}_{\gtrsim N}u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}L^{2}_{x}}^{2}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}\\
&\lesssim T^{\frac{1}{4}}\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}\big[\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}^{3}+\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{s-\frac{3}{4}+}_{x}}\big].\end{split}

(3.26)

Thus, combining (
3.24
), (
3.25
), and (
3.26
), we conclude that

\displaystyle\bigg(\sum_{N}N^{2s}\text{I \hskip-8.25128pt I \hskip-8.25128pt I}_{N}^{2}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim T^{\frac{1}{4}}\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}^{2}\big[\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}^{3}+\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\max(\frac{1}{2},s-\frac{3}{4}+)}_{x}}\big].

(3.27)

For the second term in (
3.21
